# Reading log: how good writers write (started 27 Oct 2026)

AI-generated (Claude Code) voice study, not for submission. Ray, 27 Oct: read 500 to 1,000 pieces, and read them for
**voice, tone, rhythm and structure**, not for content ("you know enough knowledge ... you express it poorly").
Insights come second.

**How each piece is read.** This environment's network blocks direct downloads, so every piece goes through a web
reader that returns short quotes and an analysis, not the full text. Each entry is labelled with its depth:
- `reader`: read through the web reader (first, best and last lines, rhythm, stance, structure). Quotes are short and
  come through the reader; check them against the source before reusing one.
- `full`: the whole text was read (Ahaan's essays only, so far).
- `partial` / `second-hand`: only part of the piece, or another site quoting it.

**Pace.** The web reader allows about 100 fetches an hour across this whole account, so the log grows in batches,
about 60-90 pieces an hour at best. Collections (memo anthologies, letter compilations) give several pieces per fetch.

Each entry: **Open / Best / Close** (quoted lines), **Craft** (rhythm, stance, shape), **Steal** (one move worth
copying), **Insight** (only if there is one). What the entries add up to goes in `SYNTHESIS.md` after every 100.

Count so far: **765**

## Round 2 (from 27 Oct evening)

### 020 · Paul Graham, "How to Make Wealth" · essay · reader
http://www.paulgraham.com/wealth.html
- Open: "If you wanted to get rich, how would you do it?" · Close: "Let the nerds keep their lunch money, and you rule the world."
- Craft: medium sentences cut by short punches; bold section heads, one idea each; "you" throughout; dry and rude in places. Opponent: the "Pie Fallacy".
- Steal: a back-of-envelope sum built from named multipliers, then admit it is rough while defending its shape.
- Insight: you get rich through your own work only where the work is measured and has leverage.

### 021 · Paul Graham, "How to Disagree" · essay · reader
http://www.paulgraham.com/disagree.html
- Open: "The web is turning writing into a conversation." · Best: "Better that than grave and wrong."
- Craft: thesis, then a graded ladder (DH0 to DH6) with an example on every rung. Calm "I".
- Steal: a named ladder lets the reader place their own objection on it.
- Insight: a ranking caps how persuasive an argument can be; it doesn't make it right.

### 022 · Paul Graham, "The Age of the Essay" · essay · reader
http://www.paulgraham.com/essay.html
- Open: "Remember the essays you had to write in high school?" · Best: "At sixteen I was about as observant as a lump of rock." · Close: "And that's certainly not something I realized when I started writing this."
- Craft: one-word fragments ("Oy.") next to long stacked sentences; one-word bold heads (Trying, The River, Surprise). Opponent: the school essay that defends a fixed position.
- Steal: the ending admits the essay changed the writer's mind while writing it.
- Insight: an essay is a trial, written to find out; write what surprised you.

### 023 · Paul Graham, "What You'll Wish You'd Known" · speech-essay · reader
http://www.paulgraham.com/hs.html
- Open: "When I said I was speaking at a high school, my friends were curious." · Best: "Rebellion is almost as stupid as obedience."
- Craft: written for teenagers without talking down; picks a worn slogan and swaps in a sharper one.
- Steal: name the cliché the reader expects, then beat it.
- Insight: stay upwind; choose the path that keeps the most options open.

### 024 · Paul Graham, "Taste for Makers" · essay · reader
http://www.paulgraham.com/taste.html
- Open: "I was talking recently to a friend who teaches at MIT." · Close: "The recipe for great work is: very exacting taste, plus the ability to gratify it."
- Craft: anecdote, then the abstract question made practical; a list of bold claims, each backed by examples from several fields; one-word paragraphs ("Smack!").
- Steal: ending on a recipe with exactly two ingredients.
- Insight: if taste can improve, "taste is subjective" can't be wholly true.

### 025 · Paul Graham, "What You Can't Say" · essay · reader
http://www.paulgraham.com/say.html
- Open: "Have you ever seen an old photo of yourself and been embarrassed at the way you looked?" · Best: "Argue with idiots, and you become an idiot." · Close: "What can't you say? And why?"
- Craft: universal memory as the hook, then turned on the reader; the close echoes the title as two short questions.
- Steal: circle back: the last line answers or re-asks the first.

### 026 · Paul Graham, "The Top Idea in Your Mind" · essay · reader
http://www.paulgraham.com/top.html
- Open: "I realized recently that what one thinks about in the shower in the morning is more important than I'd thought." · Best: "My wife thinks I'm more forgiving than she is, but my motives are purely selfish."
- Craft: anecdote, principle, two examples, self-test; the confession is funny because it is against interest.
- Steal: end with a test the reader can run tonight.

### 027 · Paul Graham, "Stuff" · essay · reader
http://www.paulgraham.com/stuff.html
- Open: "I have too much stuff." · Close: "Imagine walking around for years with five pound ankle weights, then suddenly having them removed."
- Craft: a four-word opening confession; long paragraphs with one-line punches ("And pow, more stuff."); ends on a physical image, not a moral.
- Steal: close on a body sensation the reader can feel instead of a summary.
- Insight: an asset is worth what you get from using it; unused things are worth little, whatever they cost.

### 028-039 · Howard Marks, early memos 1990-1997 (Oaktree, "The Complete Collection") · investor memos · reader
https://www.oaktreecapital.com/docs/default-source/memos/the-complete-collection.pdf
| # | Memo | Open | Best line | Steal |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| 028 | The Route to Performance (Oct 1990) | "We all seek investment performance which is above average, but how to achieve it remains a major question." | "if you can avoid losers (and losing years), the winners will take care of themselves." | Prove the thesis with someone else's track record, then state the lesson flat. |
| 029 | First Quarter Performance (Apr 1991) | "The mood swings of the securities markets resemble the movement of a pendulum." | "it is the movement toward an extreme itself that supplies the energy for the swing back." | One physical image in the first line, returned to all the way through. |
| 030 | Microeconomics 101: Supply, Demand and Convertibles (Oct 1992) | "Two principal factors determine whether an investment will be successful." | "buying any asset for less than it's worth virtually assures success." | Define jargon in brackets mid-sentence, with a joke, so the reader never stops. |
| 031 | The Value of Predictions, or Where'd All This Rain Come From? (Feb 1993) | "Anyone who has been my client for long has heard from me on many occasions with negative comments about market forecasts." | "Extreme predictions are rarely right, but they're the ones that make you big money." | A weather subtitle frames the piece; bold claims, each with a short proof. |
| 032 | Random Thoughts on the Identification of Investment Opportunities (Jan 1994) | "No group or sector in the investment world enjoys as its birthright the promise of consistent high returns." | "Investing is a popularity contest, and the most dangerous thing is to buy something at the peak of its popularity." | Numbered maxims, one example each. |
| 033 | Risk in Today's Markets (Feb 1994) | Opens on one day's harsh market reaction to a small rate rise. | "almost every sword is two-edged, and he who lives by a risky strategy may die by it." | Describe a trend by listing what people are doing, then ask the reader to check their own exposure. |
| 034 | "Risk in Today's Markets" Revisited (Apr 1994) | "Seven weeks ago, we put out a memorandum entitled 'Risk in Today's Markets.'" | "Investment actions predicated on everything continuing to go well are bound to fail." | Go back to your own earlier call, say what happened, pull out the lessons. |
| 035 | How Does an Inefficient Market Get That Way? (Jul 1994) | Defines the efficient market first. | "Investors fail to act objectively and dispassionately." | Describe the ideal, then show why reality misses it with one case. |
| 036 | How the Game Should Be Played (May 1995) | A question people kept asking him. | "our philosophy mandates that we put the greatest emphasis on trying to avoid losing our clients' money." | Origin story, then a sports metaphor, then rules as a short numbered list. |
| 037 | The Value of Predictions II, or "Give That Man a Cigar" (Jul 1996) | Recalls his own 1993 memo. | "it's possible to be right about the macro-future once in a while, but not on a regular basis." | Test your old claim with your own small data set and admit the sample's limits first. |
| 038 | Will It Be Different This Time? (Nov 1996) | Opens on a favourite newspaper article. | "But I recoil any time I hear a prediction that trees will grow to the sky, or that centuries of history are irrelevant." | Lay out the other side's case in full, then answer each claim with history. |
| 039 | Are You an Investor or a Speculator? (1997; partial) | "All of Oaktree's activities follow from our conviction that..." | "We think speculating, according to this definition, is more prudent than investing." | Quote a famous definition, then turn its usual meaning upside down. |

Craft across the twelve: Marks writes the same way for 35 years. A plain, almost dull first sentence; one image or one
definition; numbered or bolded claims; a self-deprecating admission; no adjectives doing the work. **His voice is his
opinions repeated with patience, not his sentences.** Caplet's use: the memo-to-self ("Revisited") shape suits the
Final Report: go back to the IPS's own claims and grade them.

### 040-044 · Nick Sleep, Nomad Investment Partnership letters 2002-2003 · investor letters · reader
https://suredividend.com/wp-content/uploads/2024/11/Nomad-Letters.pdf
| # | Letter | Open / shape | Best line | Steal |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| 040 | Inaugural letter, 18 Jan 2002 | "The Nomad Investment Partnership was launched in early September 2001 and began investing on September 10th." The date is left to do its own work. | "Finding all three is rare, and that is why we think Nomad has a material advantage in being a global fund." | Thesis as a three-part checklist; the rarity of meeting all three *is* the edge. |
| 041 | Interim, 30 Jun 2002 | Results first, against the index. | "You can bank on us being the buyer." | After a long, evidence-heavy paragraph, one blunt line in the reader's terms. |
| 042 | Year end, 31 Dec 2002 | Results first, then admits a down period is unpleasant to report. | "Investment mistakes are inevitable and indeed to some extent desirable." | Admit the failure plainly, then make it a point about process. |
| 043 | Interim, 30 Jun 2003 | Results first. | Quotes Groucho Marx: "I do not care to belong to any club that would accept me as a member." | A famous line from an unexpected source, used as a sparring partner. |
| 044 | Annual, 31 Dec 2003 (partial) | "There are two ways to present results: either in discrete annual increments or on a compounded basis." | "If we ever write to you asking for permission to invest in any of the above, sell your shares." | Deadpan escalation to a flat order the reader could act on. |

Craft: British understatement, long patient paragraphs, then a short line with a smile in it. Sleep treats his
partners as intelligent adults and his own mistakes as data. Caplet's use: the deadpan order ("if we ever ...,
sell") is a way to state a rule with teeth, e.g. if Caplet ever sells a rung before its date, something has gone wrong.

### 045-048 · Cliff Asness and AQR, "Cliff's Perspectives" · investor essays · reader
| # | Piece | Open | Best line | Steal | Insight |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 045 | "Buffer Madness" https://www.aqr.com/Insights/Perspectives/Buffer-Madness | "Last month, we posted 'Rebuffed,' a short piece critical of options-based strategies..." | "Spoiler: it's the placebo effect." | State each objection at its strongest, then answer it with an exhibit. | Judge any product against the simple mix an investor could hold instead. |
| 046 | "(So) What If You Miss the Market's N Best Days?" https://www.aqr.com/Insights/Perspectives/So-What-If-You-Miss-the-Markets-N-Best-Days | "A mere 25+ years ago, I submitted a paper to the Financial Analysts Journal." | "If you're going to make very, very bad arguments, you could at least use a modicum of creativity!" | Open on your own rejection story to earn trust before you argue. | Missing the best days mirrors missing the worst; the scare stat is one-sided. |
| 047 | "2035: An Allocator Looks Back Over the Last 10 Years" https://www.aqr.com/Insights/Perspectives/2035-An-Allocator-Looks-Back-Over-the-Last-10-Years | "Well, sitting here in the year 2035 and looking back at our endowment's returns for the last decade is not a pleasant task." | "Fool us twice, shame on we!" | Write from the future as a confession; footnotes as a second, sardonic voice. | Smooth marks hide risk; paying for comfort lowers future returns. |
| 048 | "There Ain't No Such Thing as a Free Lunch" https://www.aqr.com/Insights/Perspectives/There-Aint-No-Such-Thing-as-a-Free-Lunch | "Being an equity investor is hard." | "A lot of people (apparently especially, uh ok, boomers) really seem to like these funds." | Answer the reader's question in one word ("Nope.") before explaining. | A buffer fund's protection is paid for with capped upside, option costs and fees. |

Craft: Asness is the funniest serious writer in the reading so far, and the jokes are always aimed at an argument,
never decoration. Brackets talk back to the main sentence. **For Caplet (insight):** #048 is the case against the
product a bank would sell Laura. Caplet's "capital-protected note built at home" keeps the upside a buffer fund caps
and pays no option premium: the same promise without the fee. Idea worth a Writing Room trial.

### 049 · Nick Sleep, Nomad interim letter, Jun 2004 · investor letter · reader
- Open: "Nomad's year to date performance is sufficiently close to the index..." · Best: "Investment dollars work best when they occupy a different psychic space to almost any other form of savings you may have."
- Steal: a serious point undercut by a dry bracket ("(and certainly not a hedge fund!)").
- Insight for Caplet: Laura's ladder money lives in a different "psychic space" from her stock money, and the IPS can say so: it is spoken for.

### 050-054 · Morgan Housel and Collaborative Fund, Substack · blog · reader
| # | Piece | Open | Best line | Steal |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| 050 | Housel, "Pessimism always sounds smarter than optimism" https://collabfund.substack.com/p/pessimism-always-sounds-smarter-than | "A few quotes, ideas, and links I don't want to forget." | "The cost of debt is not the interest rate you pay." | Name a familiar cost, then redefine it as what you actually lose. |
| 051 | Housel, "Plan on the plan not going according to plan" https://collabfund.substack.com/p/plan-on-the-plan-not-going-according | Same digest opener. | "Progress happens too slowly to notice, but setbacks happen too quickly to ignore." | A sentence that turns on itself (slowly/quickly, notice/ignore). |
| 052 | Housel, "Long-Term Money" https://collabfund.substack.com/p/long-term-money | Opens on Adam Smith and a Highland mother. | "Like wealth, there is no objective definition of what counts as spoiled – everything is just relative to someone else." | A stepped list of worries, one per line, each a generation up. Ends: "Which is what you and I are today." |
| 053 | Collaborative Fund, "What Doesn't Change" https://collabfund.substack.com/p/same-as-it-ever-was | "Every era tells itself a story about what's different this time..." | "Consider this a reminder of what's durable when everything feels anything but." | A list of dated quotes as the argument: the dates do the persuading. |
| 054 | Housel, "Brave Enough To Invest for the Long Run" https://collabfund.substack.com/p/brave-enough-to-invest-for-the-long | "The proper financial mindset is to be scared enough to save for the short run and brave enough to invest for the long run." | "Money you didn't earn or work hard for quickly becomes a social liability." | Two emotions mapped onto two time horizons in one parallel sentence. |

Craft: Housel's whole voice is the mirrored sentence (slowly/quickly; scared/brave), and an old story told as if it
were yesterday's. **Caplet's use (#054 is almost Caplet's thesis):** "scared enough to save for the short run, brave
enough to invest for the long run" maps onto Laura's ladder and her stocks. A judge who knows Housel will hear the
echo, so Caplet must not borrow the line; the *shape* (two feelings, two clocks) is free to use.

### 055-062 · Howard Marks, memos 2001-2023 · investor memos · reader
Index: https://www.oaktreecapital.com/insights/memos
| # | Memo | Open | Best line | Steal |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| 055 | You Can't Predict. You Can Prepare. (Nov 2001) | "For the title of this memo I've borrowed the tagline from Mass Mutual's advertising campaign." | "Either way's fine – just don't tell me it'll be a straight line." | Put the reader's objection in quote marks, grant it, then reframe. |
| 056 | You Can't Eat IRR (Jul 2006) | "Until rather recently – certainly up to the early 1980s – 'investing' was largely synonymous with 'stocks and bonds.'" | "Reliance on a single figure can't possibly provide the answer – not even IRR." (close) | A title that punctures jargon in plain words; worked examples let the reader find the flaw before he names it. |
| 057 | Risk (Jan 2006) | Opens on reading papers for a board he sits on. | "Why not? It's simple: if riskier investments reliably produced higher returns, they wouldn't be riskier!" | A loaded question answered with one blunt sentence. |
| 058 | Plan B (Sep 2008) | "Over the last decade or two, Plan A consisted of relying on the free market..." | "Take a few hundred or thousand of me, and you have a run on the bank." | Explain a system's failure through what *he* did with his own money; one-word jabs ("Oops?"). |
| 059 | The Limits to Negativism (Oct 2008) | "The markets acted on Monday as if the credit crisis is behind us..." | "When others love 'em, we should hate 'em. But when others hate 'em, we can love 'em." | A mirrored pair of sentences, in slang, carrying the whole philosophy. |
| 060 | Dare to Be Great II (Apr 2014) | Recalls his own 2006 memo. | "Being too far ahead of your time is indistinguishable from being wrong." | A homely sports or poker story, then a crisp line to end it. |
| 061 | Liquidity (Mar 2015) | "My wife Nancy's accusations of repetitiveness notwithstanding, once in a while I think of something..." | "When you can't meet a margin call because you have insufficient liquidity, that's profound." (close) | Disarm with a domestic joke before a technical argument. |
| 062 | The Folly of Certainty (2023) https://www.oaktreecapital.com/insights/memo/the-folly-of-certainty | "The impetus for my memos can come from a wide variety of sources." | "So, if anything, it reinforces my bottom line: making predictions is largely a loser's game." (close) | One small trigger (a talk he heard) turned into a broad principle. |

Craft: Marks's humour is almost always at his own expense (his wife, his repetitiveness, "a few thousand of me").
Self-mockery buys the right to be blunt about everyone else. **Caplet's use:** #061 is directly useful. "It's often a
mistake to say a particular asset is either liquid or illiquid": Laura's rungs are illiquid only to someone who wants
the money before its date, and she doesn't.

## Strand: pitches, founder letters, speeches (Ray, 27 Oct: "judges are Shark Tank investors, not examiners")
These are read for one thing: **how they move a reader who holds the money**. Template: Open / the line where the
feeling lands (Turn) / Close / which emotion and how / what makes the listener believe / Steal.

| # | Piece | Type | Open | Turn | Close | How it moves | Steal |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 063 | Steve Jobs, Stanford commencement (2005) https://www.rev.com/blog/transcripts/steve-jobs-stanford-commencement-speech-transcript-2005 | speech | "Thank you. I'm honored to be with you today..." | The doctor crying because the cancer is the rare curable kind. | "Stay hungry, stay foolish. Thank you all very much." | Dread, then relief; exact details (a 7:30 a.m. scan, returning Coke bottles for food) make it lived, not preached. | One true personal story, and one short line the listener can repeat. |
| 064 | Sequoia, "Writing a Business Plan" https://sequoiacap.com/article/writing-a-business-plan | VC guide | Airbnb's founders "had an air mattress, entrepreneurial passion, and a vision" | "We love partnering with founders hell-bent on bringing an idea to life that conventional wisdom deems impossible." | - | Order: purpose, problem, solution, why now, market, competition, model, team, financials, vision. | Say what you are in one sentence before any feature. |
| 065 | Linas Beliūnas, "The most powerful pitch deck framework" https://linas.substack.com/p/pitchdeckframework | Substack | "Every great entrepreneur is someone who can tell a compelling story." | - | (a plug) | **Counter-example.** It tells you to tell stories and never tells one. Checklist voice. | The lesson is the gap: advice about emotion has none. |
| 066 | Larry Page and Sergey Brin, Google IPO letter, "An Owner's Manual" (2004) https://aletteraday.substack.com/p/letter-96-larry-page-and-sergey-brin | founder letter | "Google is not a conventional company." | Quarterly targets are "as pointless as a dieter stepping on a scale every half hour". | "We wrote this letter to help you understand our company." | Candour: it lists the downsides the investor must accept, then the things the founders will refuse to do. | Name what you will *not* do, and the cost of that refusal to the reader. |
| 067 | Yvon Chouinard, "Earth is now our only shareholder" (2022) https://www.patagonia.ca/fr/stories/planet/activism/a-letter-from-yvon-chouinard/story-127258.html | founder letter | "Earth is now our only shareholder." | "But it's also resilient. We can save our planet if we commit to it." | Same line, repeated. | Unease (our efforts are "not enough"), then hope; "I never wanted to be a businessman" makes him a reluctant, trusted narrator; the trust mechanics make it real. | Reject the alternatives one by one, then repeat one line so the choice feels inevitable. |
| 068 | Brian Chesky, "Don't fuck up the culture" (2014) https://www.startups.com/library/founder-stories/brian-chesky-culture | founder letter | "Our next team meeting is dedicated to Core Values..." (deliberately dull) | Peter Thiel's five-word advice, quoted raw. | "But culture is forever." | The dull opening sets up the jolt; fear of losing something, then shared ownership. | Start flat on purpose so the surprise lands. |
| 069 | John F. Kennedy, Rice University, "We choose to go to the Moon" (1962) https://www.rev.com/blog/transcripts/john-f-kennedy-jfk-moon-speech-transcript-we-choose-to-go-to-the-moon | speech | "We meet in an hour of change and challenge, in a decade of hope and fear, in an age of both knowledge and ignorance." | "...not because they are easy, but because they are hard." | Asks blessing on "the most hazardous and dangerous and greatest adventure..." | Squeezes 50,000 years of history into 50 so progress feels dizzying; exact numbers (240,000 miles, a 300-foot rocket) make the dream physical. | Compress a long timeline into a human span; make the goal a *choice*, not a duty. |
| 070 | Martin Luther King Jr., "I Have a Dream" (1963), via the American Bar Association's rhetorical analysis https://www.americanbar.org/groups/litigation/resources/litigation-journal/2022-summer/successful-rhetoric-of-i-have-dream-speech | speech | "100 years later" (anaphora) | "So we've come to cash this check, a check that will give us upon demand the riches of freedom, and the security of justice." | "I have a dream" (epistrophe) | **A financial metaphor carrying a moral claim.** The crowd holds a cheque already written, so they are claimants, not beggars; the calm banking words discipline the anger. | End a sentence on a loaded word and start the next with it. |
| 071 | Mark Zuckerberg, Facebook IPO letter, "The Hacker Way" (2012) https://www.hypebot.com/full-text-of-mark-zuckerbergs-letter-to-potential-facebook-investors/ | founder letter | "Facebook was not originally created to be a company." | "Simply put: we don't build services to make money; we make money to build better services." | "I look forward to building something great together." | Cause before company; the chiasmus makes profit a by-product of the mission. | Open on the origin, then invert the reader's expected order of means and ends. |

**First read of the strand (for the voice guide's emotion section):** nobody here *names* an emotion. They put the
reader somewhere (a doctor's office, a stadium in Houston, a cheque in your hand) and let the feeling arrive. The
conviction comes from a refusal or a cost the speaker accepts in public (Google won't guide earnings; Chouinard gives
the company away; Kennedy picks the hard thing).

**For Caplet, #070 is the find of the day.** King's whole speech runs on a promissory note: a cheque that came back
marked "insufficient funds". Laura is writing ten cheques to artists she has not met, dated 2033 to 2042. Caplet's job
is that none of them bounce. The bad-cheque image is too famous to borrow, but the *move* (a financial promise as a
moral one, the reader holding the paper) is exactly the emotional line the IPS lacks: the payments are not
"liabilities", they are promises with someone's name on them.

### 072-076 · Warren Buffett, Berkshire letters · company letters · reader
| # | Letter | Best line | Close | Steal |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| 072 | 2014 (50th anniversary) http://www.berkshirehathaway.com/letters/2014ltr.pdf | "It's better to have a partial interest in the Hope Diamond than to own all of a rhinestone." | - | The anniversary section opens on a confession: in 1964 he turned down an offer over an eighth of a point, "a monumentally stupid decision". A milestone told as an embarrassing memory. |
| 073 | 2002 http://www.berkshirehathaway.com/letters/2002pdf.pdf | "Unlike LBO operators and private equity firms, we have no 'exit' strategy . . . we buy to keep." | "Again, please vote only if there is a reasonable chance that you will be attending some meetings in the future." | Numbers first, then the serious point carried by a folksy story. |
| 074 | 2016 http://www.berkshirehathaway.com/letters/2016ltr.pdf | "Every decade or so, dark clouds will fill the economic skies, and they will briefly rain gold." | "Show you are a sophisticated diner by ordering the T-bone with hash browns." | A homely image for a big principle, then move on fast. The voice never breaks, even in the logistics. |
| 075 | 2023 (Charlie Munger tribute) http://www.berkshirehathaway.com/letters/2023ltr.pdf | "Charlie never sought to take credit for his role as creator but instead let me take the bows and receive the accolades." | "...Charlie should forever be credited with being the architect." | Opens with a date and a number: "Charlie Munger died on November 28, just 33 days before his 100th birthday." Grief carried by restraint and one building metaphor (architect vs construction crew). |
| 076 | 2010 http://www.berkshirehathaway.com/letters/2010ltr.pdf | "We would rather be approximately right than precisely wrong." | "Come to our Woodstock for Capitalism on April 30th and tell them so." | A broad claim pinned to one image, then a flat short sentence. |

Craft: every Buffett letter opens on the dullest possible sentence (the year's change in book value) and earns
everything after it. **Caplet's use:** #073's "we buy to keep" is the cleanest statement of Laura's ladder in anyone's
words: no exit strategy, because the exit is the date. #075 shows the most moving sentence in the reading so far is a
date and a number ("33 days before his 100th birthday"); a number can carry grief if it measures something a person
lost or nearly had.

### 077-079 · Essays and a speech · reader / second-hand
| # | Piece | Open | Best / Turn | Close | Steal |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 077 | J. M. Keynes, "Economic Possibilities for our Grandchildren" (1930) https://www.hetwebsite.net/het/texts/keynes/keynes1930grandchildren.htm | "We are suffering just now from a bad attack of economic pessimism." | "Thus by pushing his jam always forward into the future, he strives to secure for his act of boiling it an immortality." | "If economists could manage to get themselves thought of as humble, competent people, on a level with dentists, that would be splendid!" | A grand forecast carried by a homely image (jam). The great economist ends by wishing his profession were as useful as dentists. Ironic self-demotion as the kicker. Insight: small rates over long horizons become vast sums. |
| 078 | David Foster Wallace, "This Is Water" (Kenyon, 2005) https://fs.blog/david-foster-wallace-this-is-water/ | "There are these two young fish swimming along..." | "The capital-T Truth is about life BEFORE death." | "This is water." | Disarm authority first (he says he is not the wise old fish), then a concrete supermarket queue that makes the dread personal; the parable comes back as the last three words. |
| 079 | George Orwell, "Shooting an Elephant" (1936), second-hand via https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shooting_an_Elephant | "I was hated by large numbers of people—the only time in my life that I have been important enough for this to happen to me." | Shot it "solely to avoid looking a fool." | (not reached) | A first line that confesses and jokes at once; the crowd as silent pressure. |

### 080-087 · John Locke Institute prize essays (Ahaan's genre) · student essays · reader
Index: https://johnlocke.counselorjay.com/essays.html (files on johnlockeinstitute.com)
| # | Question (year) | Open | Best line | Close | Steal |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 080 | Government funded only by new central-bank money? (Economics, 2023) | "Government spending on hospitals, school, roads and the rest is funded from taxation or government borrowing." | "Think of 'printing money', and the hyperinflation of Zimbabwe and Weimar Germany come to mind." | Verdict, then grants money creation a narrow role in downturns. | Give the other case its strongest form, "Of course...", then "Despite this". |
| 081 | What makes social movements succeed? (History, 2023) | Repeats the question. | "Again, by Western theory, this social movement achieved little change." | "Only then can we truly understand the multifarious nature of social movements..." | Restate the opponent's yardstick in its own words, then flip it with one pivot word. |
| 082 | Judge the past by today's standards? (History, 2023) | Repeats the question. | "An illusory 'peace of the past' implies an ethical relativism that is more dangerous than judging the past." | "Let us hope that they approach us with sufficient empathy." | Ends by turning the question on the reader's own era. |
| 083 | "Innocent until proven guilty" for public censure? (Law, 2023) | "In almost all civilized criminal justice systems, few maxims have received more widespread adoption..." | "The implication is not that anyone in the public spotlight should be deprived of any interest in their reputation." | "...illogical, unjustifiable, and impractical." | Say what you are *not* claiming before the reader can object. A triad to close. |
| 084 | Should law stop self-harming choices? (Law, 2023) | Repeats the question. | "Life is worth living because I get to decide how I live it, not some stranger in the government." | "If it does not, it can be restrained." | Every principle anchored in an everyday case ("When I dine at a restaurant..."). The "I" keeps philosophy human. |
| 085 | Are beliefs voluntary? (Philosophy, 2023) | Opens on a dictionary definition. | (an "ergo" hinge) | "...belief is involuntary." | **Counter-example:** a dictionary opening and dense citations. It won on argument, not voice. |
| 086 | Can you believe what you can't persuade friends of? (Theology, 2023) | "In the Sanskrit epic, the Mahābhārata, the archer prince Arjuna has a religious revelation in the midst of a war." | "Why should he give his own judgement more weight than people who are just as intelligent as he is?" | "I may still have enough reason to believe what I believe." | One concrete story first, then the question it raises; short questions between long sentences. |
| 087 | Who should own your data? (Junior, 2020) https://www.johnlockeinstitute.com/2020-first-prize-junior-essay | "Today, we produce unfathomable amounts of data..." | "Digital monopolies have a data-supported 'God's eye' view of their own market." | "...res nullius - 'property of no one.'" | One headed section per option: fair concession, then "Unfortunately,". |

**What the winners share, and where Ahaan is already ahead:** the winners are formal, fair to the other side,
heavily signposted ("Firstly ... However ... Despite this"), and only lightly witty. Their edge is argument and fairness,
not voice. Ahaan's essays have the same fairness plus much more voice (puns, kickers, short punches). So "write like
the John Locke winners" would make Caplet *less* distinctive; the thing to copy from them is the fair concession and
the "what I am not claiming" sentence. The best opener here (#086) starts in a story, not on the question.

### 088-090 · Johns Hopkins "Essays That Worked" (class of 2028) · student essays · reader
| # | Essay | Open | Turn | Close | Note |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 088 | "Korean Sticky Notes" https://apply.jhu.edu/hopkins-insider/korean-sticky-notes/ | "A myriad of sticky notes adorn our bedroom door, each bearing a telling Korean word." | "Looking back, my mom's words revealed an undeniable truth." | (moves beyond the "financial advisor" role at home) | The structure is the vocabulary: each section is a Korean word. A price ("$9.99 for five mangoes") carries the feeling. |
| 089 | "Being the Handyman" https://apply.jhu.edu/hopkins-insider/being-the-handyman/ | "I've been the 'handyman' of the house for as long as I can remember." | Carpentry arrives "thanks to our two large dogs, Jake and Elwood." | "...a resilient spirit that thrives on the joy of learning through hands-on experience." | Escalating objects (tape, sewing, fence boards, a truck). **The close is plastic.** |
| 090 | "The Art of Imperfection" https://apply.jhu.edu/hopkins-insider/the-art-of-imperfection/ | "The splash of color that engulfed the wooden tables and bar stools..." | "The hurdle I needed to overcome was not the task itself" | "...the confidence to view challenges as opportunities for growth." | Strong sensory start, **plastic close**. |

Lesson: even the essays that worked lose their voice in the last line, where the writer starts *summarising what they
learned*. The openings win; the closes go generic. Caplet's kicker rule (end on a thing, not a lesson) comes from this.

### 091-094 · Michael Cembalest, J.P. Morgan "Eye on the Market" · bank research · reader
| # | Piece | Open | Best line | Steal |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| 091 | "Outlook 2024: Pillow Talk" https://am.jpmorgan.com/content/dam/jpm-am-aem/global/en/insights/eye-on-the-market/outlook-2024-amv.pdf | "Falling US inflation and prospects for easier Fed policy are increasing talk of a soft landing..." | "The Fed has enough egg on its face already." | A pun title ("soft landing", pillow) and section heads with a metaphor plus a dry subtitle ("The Boiling Frog"). Charts arrive behind a plain claim and "As shown below". |
| 092 | "Market Outlook 2023: The End of the Affair" https://am.jpmorgan.com/content/dam/jpm-am-aem/global/en/insights/market-insights/eotm/2023-outlook-amv.pdf | "The affair with the market catalysts of the last decade is over now, and a new era of investing begins." | "I also believe the moon landing was real since I watched it live on TV when I was 7." | A whole market regime told as a break-up. Each chart opens with its verdict as the headline. |
| 093 | "Heliocentrism: Objects may be further away than they appear" https://am.jpmorgan.com/content/dam/jpm-am-aem/global/en/insights/eye-on-the-market/heliocentrism-amv.pdf | "The renewable transition exists in the eye of the beholder." | "Jacobson's methodology is the equivalent of a vegan who swears off meat but only in between meals." | Puncture an expert's claim with an everyday analogy; the fine print goes to a footnote. The jokes do the rebutting. |
| 094 | "Outlook 2022: Reflation: Endgame" https://am.jpmorgan.com/content/dam/jpm-am-aem/global/en/insights/market-insights/eotm/reflation-endgame-amv.pdf | "Greetings and Happy New Year." | "While I have never seen a superhero movie, their marketing posters gave us the idea for the cover." | A blockbuster title on dry economics; a footnote confession (he failed the firm's phishing test) as a second, candid voice. |

Craft: Cembalest's research is the most "voiced" bank writing in the reading. The trick is **two registers on one
page**: the body is sober and chart-led; the titles, brackets and footnotes are where the person lives. Caplet's
IPS can't have footnotes, but the Final Report can.

### 095-097 · Aswath Damodaran, "Musings on Markets" (Substack) · blog · reader
| # | Piece | Open | Best line | Steal | Insight |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 095 | "A Moody's Ratings Downgrade for the US: What now?" https://aswathdamodaran.substack.com/p/a-moodys-ratings-downgrade-for-the | "I was on a family vacation in August 2011 when I received an email from a journalist asking me what I thought about the S&P..." | "Since I stay blissfully unaware of most news stories and things related to markets when I am on the beach..." | Open on a dated personal scene, then the market question. Numbers chained so each feeds the next. | Treasury yields barely moved: a downgrade mostly writes down a default spread markets already priced. Useful for Caplet's "residual risk = US default" line. |
| 096 | "Alternative Investments: Big Promises, Questionable Payoffs!" https://aswathdamodaran.substack.com/p/alternative-investments-big-promises | "It is true that most investing lessons are directed at those who invest only in stocks and bonds..." | "At the risk of raising the ire of crypto-enthusiasts..." | Concede each sales pitch in its own words, then audit it. | Appraisal lag makes private assets look less correlated than they are. |
| 097 | "Buy the Dip! The Appeal and Dangers in Contrarian Investing!" https://aswathdamodaran.substack.com/p/buy-the-dip-the-appeal-and-dangers | "When markets are in free fall, there is a great deal of advice..." | "Put simply, most great companies are neutral or even bad investments, because the market prices them to be great." | Jargon translated inline: "marginal incremental returns (an academic code word for really small)". Ends by disclosing his own live orders: "the order is good until canceled!" | Skin in the game makes an argument testable. |

Craft: the professor's voice: patient, long sentences, exclamation marks in titles, and a bracket that translates the
jargon. **Steal for Caplet:** the inline translation in brackets is the best tool yet for the IPS's two or three
technical words (pull to par, duration): say it, then say what it means in five words, in brackets.

### 098-099 · Matt Levine; Evan Armstrong on Kyla Scanlon · columns · reader
| # | Piece | Open | Best line | Steal |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| 098 | Matt Levine, "We Has Some Bonds to Sell" (Money Stuff, Oct 2019) https://www.bloomberg.com/opinion/newsletters/2019-10-16/money-stuff-we-has-some-bonds-to-sell | "There isn't really any new news in the WeWork saga, but there are a lot of people who have waited patiently..." | "Good good. Vicki Bryan do you have anything to add?" | Admit there's no news, then make the thin update a favour to the people queueing to mock; end on a stage cue. |
| 099 | Evan Armstrong, "Vibes Are a Legitimate Economic Indicator" (Every) https://every.to/napkin-math/vibes-are-a-legitimate-economic-indicator | "Kyla Scanlon is good at the internet." | "things have been, like, kinda weird over the last decade." | A wink in brackets "(ha!)" straight after a key claim: personality without derailing. |

### 100 · Jeff Bezos, Amazon's 1997 letter to shareholders · company letter · reader
https://www.aboutamazon.com/md/news/company-news/amazons-original-1997-letter-to-shareholders
- Open: "Amazon.com passed many milestones in 1997..." · Best: "We believe that a fundamental measure of our success will be the shareholder value we create over the long term."
- Craft: plain sentences; numbers do the persuading (1.5 million customers, revenue up 838%); short headings ("It's All About the Long Term"); the commitments list repeats one stem ("We will ...") so conviction builds, and it admits what the commitments will cost. Disarms with "We aren't so bold as to claim...".
- Steal: a list of promises with one repeated stem, each paired with the price the reader will pay for it.

## Pieces 101-200 (night of 27 Oct)

### 101-105 · Classic investing texts and speeches · reader
| # | Piece | Open | Best line | Close | Steal |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 101 | Warren Buffett, "The Superinvestors of Graham-and-Doddsville" (1984) https://www.lapasserelle.com/courses/advanced_finance/documents/TheSuperinvestorsofGraham-and-Doddsville-WarrenE.Buffett.pdf | Opens on the question: is the margin-of-safety approach outdated? | "215 egotistical orangutans with 20 straight winning flips." | "...those who read their Graham & Dodd will continue to prosper." | A national coin-flipping contest makes luck look like genius; swapping in orangutans exposes the flaw. Then: what if the winners all came from one village? An absurd parallel does the rebuttal. |
| 102 | William Sharpe, "The Arithmetic of Active Management" (1991) https://marottaonmoney.com/wp-content/uploads/2021/07/The-Arithmetic-of-Active-Management.pdf | "Today's fad is index funds that track the Standard and Poor's 500." | "Security analysis (e.g. the graduates of prestigious business schools) must eat, and so must brokers, traders, specialists and other market-makers." | (benchmark chosen in advance) | A proof in three steps, then restated; dry sarcasm ("the laws of arithmetic have been suspended"). Shows that a short, rude, correct paper outlives long polite ones. |
| 103 | Bridgewater, "50 Years of the Daily Observations" https://www.bridgewater.com/50-years-of-the-bridgewater-daily-observations (partial) | On the firm's 50th anniversary release. | "There are times when big shifts happen and the right framework is crucial..." | - | Each reprinted piece is graded against what later happened. Accountability as a structure. |
| 104 | Ray Dalio, "How the Economic Machine Works" (page) https://economicprinciples.org/how-the-economic-machine-works (partial) | "This simple but not simplistic video..." | "Identify the paradigm you're in, examine if and how it is unsustainable" | - | One metaphor (a machine) with a few visible parts (credit, rates, leverage) the reader already understands. |
| 105 | Charlie Munger, "The Psychology of Human Misjudgment" (1995) https://singjupost.com/full-transcript-the-psychology-of-human-misjudgement-charlie-munger/ | "Although, I am very interested in the subject of human misjudgment, and Lord knows I've created my..." | "Smart people make these terrible blunders." | "In fact, I think it's terrible." | A numbered catalogue: anecdote, then a short verdict. Mock-serious images ("man-with-a-hammer syndrome"). Opens by admitting his own misjudgments. |

### 106-112 · The great essayists · reader / second-hand
| # | Piece | Open | Best line | Close | Steal |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 106 | Joan Didion, "On Self-Respect" (1961) https://www.westridge.org/uploaded/LMS_documents/Didion,_On_Self-Respect.pdf (summary + quotes) | Opens on her own youthful failure to make Phi Beta Kappa. | People with self-respect "have the courage of their mistakes" | One who lacks it runs away "to find oneself, and finds no one at home." | A private embarrassment as the doorway to a moral argument. |
| 107 | Didion, "Fixed Opinions, or the Hinge of History" (2003) http://www.nybooks.com/articles/archives/2003/jan/16/fixed-opinions-or-the-hinge-of-history/ | "Seven days after September 11, 2001, I left New York to do two weeks of book promotion..." | "I hit the word 'perishable' and I could not say it." | - | Mundane logistics, then one word that breaks the calm. |
| 108 | Didion, "California Notes" (2016) http://www.nybooks.com/articles/2016/05/26/california-notes/ | "The first time I was ever on an airplane was in 1955 and flights had names." | "I believed that I would always go to teas." | - | A sweeping claim, then a small faintly deflating detail, left uninterpreted. |
| 109 | Didion, "Goodbye to All That" (1967), second-hand https://blogs.shu.edu/litandthecity/?p=857 | Remembers exactly when New York began for her, not when it ended. | - | - | A beginning you can date, an ending you can't: the gap drives the piece. |
| 110 | James Baldwin, "Notes of a Native Son" (1955) https://www.whatsoproudlywehail.org/wp-content/uploads/2013/08/Baldwin_Notes-of-a-Native-Son.pdf | "On the 29th of July, in 1943, my father died." | "We had got on badly, partly because we shared, in our different fashions, the vice of stubborn pride." | Wishing his father beside him to search "his face for the answers" | A date and a death in nine words. Private grief braided with public violence (the funeral passes "a wilderness of smashed plate glass"). Long periodic sentences that snap short. |
| 111 | E. B. White, "Once More to the Lake" (1941) https://hhh.gavilan.edu/ecrook/1A.fall.2005/1A.white.html | "One summer, along about 1904, my father rented a camp on a lake in Maine and took us all there for the month of August." | "There had been no years." | "As he buckled the swollen belt suddenly my groin felt the chill of death." | Warm detail for pages, then a small bodily detail that turns it. The best ending in the log so far. |
| 112 | E. B. White, "Here Is New York" (1949), partial via https://mehfil.substack.com/p/here-is-new-york-by-eb-white | - | "Prosperity creates its bread lines, the same as depression." | - | A street scene and a general truth in one breath, no lecture. |

Craft: the essayists open on **a date** (Baldwin, White, Didion x3) far more often than the investors do, and end on a
**body or an object** (White's belt, Didion's word she couldn't say). Their emotion is never named; it is placed.
This confirms guide section 7, F1 and F4. **Caplet's use:** "On the 1st of January, 2033, ..." is a Baldwin-shaped
opening for the Final Report; the IPS can't spare the words.

### 113-116 · Howard Marks, more memos · reader
| # | Memo | Open | Best line | Close | Steal |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 113 | Genius Isn't Enough (Oct 1998) | "The saga of Long-Term Capital is well known by now." | "Brilliance, like pride, often goes before the fall." | "You won't see them forgotten here." | A flat fact, then one contrast: "The answer was leverage." |
| 114 | bubble.com (Jan 2000) | Opens on Edward Chancellor's history of speculation. | "Not even one of the world's smartest men was immune to this tangible lesson in gravity!" (Newton) | Ends by quoting someone else's rosier column. | A dense quote, then a bracketed one-liner that translates it. |
| 115 | The Most Important Thing (Jul 2003) | "As I meet with clients and prospects, I repeatedly hear myself say, 'the most important thing is x.'" | "Invest scared!" | "And maybe that's the nineteenth point: never think it'll be easy." | Turn your own verbal tic into the structure (eighteen "most important things"). |
| 116 | The Long View (Jan 2009) | Looks back over his memos on 2003-07. | "But it doesn't add value or make things better; it just makes gains bigger and losses more painful." (leverage) | "This one's providing enough excitement for a lifetime." | A familiar parable organising the whole argument. |
(Also re-read 003 "Sea Change": the moving walkway is introduced as a casual aside from client visits and paid off in
the last line: "That's the sea change I'm talking about.")

### 117-122 · Paul Graham, more essays · reader
| # | Essay | Open | Best line | Close / note | Steal |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 117 | "After the Ladder" http://www.paulgraham.com/ladder.html | "Thirty years ago, one was supposed to work one's way up the corporate ladder." | "Why waste your time climbing a ladder that might disappear before you reach the top?" | "Our fathers weren't *that* stupid." | A short deflating line reframes the argument with understatement. |
| 118 | "Inequality and Risk" http://www.paulgraham.com/inequality.html | "Suppose you wanted to get rid of economic inequality." | "Like many startup founders, I did it to get rich." | Narrows to one reframed goal. | Restate a warm-sounding goal in its blunt equivalent so the hidden cost shows. |
| 119 | "Why Smart People Have Bad Ideas" http://www.paulgraham.com/bronze.html | "This summer, as an experiment, some friends and I are giving seed funding to a bunch of new startups." | "In retrospect, I wonder how we could have wasted our time on anything so stupid." | A self-mocking story becomes the thesis. | Confess your own mistake first, then generalise to the reader. |
| 120 | "Startups in 13 Sentences" http://www.paulgraham.com/13sentences.html | "One of the things I always tell startups is a principle I learned from Paul Buchheit." | "Cofounders are for a startup what location is for real estate." | "...having just ten users who love you will keep you going." | Advice anchored in a homely physical image; end on people, not a rule. |
| 121 | "How to Do What You Love" http://www.paulgraham.com/love.html | "To do something well you have to like it." | "It was like being told to use dry water." | Reassurance, not a flourish. | A plain claim and a slightly absurd comparison, so the reader feels the contradiction first. |
| 122 | "The Submarine" http://www.paulgraham.com/submarine.html | "'Suits make a corporate comeback,' says the New York Times. Why does this sound familiar?" | "If anyone is dishonest, it's the reporters." | Predicts PR will chase bloggers next. | Tie the theory to one checkable case so the reader can verify the mechanism. |

Craft across Graham's twelve: almost every essay opens in under 15 words with either a supposition ("Suppose you
wanted...") or a flat claim, and the "best line" is usually a confession or an absurd comparison ("dry water").
**Caplet's use:** "Suppose you had to promise ten people $50,000 each, starting in 2033, and could not ask anyone for
more money." That is a Graham-shaped first line for the IPS: it puts the judge in Laura's chair (F1) in 25 words.

### 123-126 · Bond kings, bank house views and forecasters · reader / second-hand
| # | Piece | Open | Best line | Steal / verdict |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| 123 | Bill Gross, PIMCO Investment Outlook on being a better investor (Apr 2013), excerpt https://www.marketfolly.com/2013/04/bill-gross-on-how-to-be-better-investor.html | "But let me admit something." | Lists the investing legends, then "Fuss, yeah – me too, have cut our teeth" on an unusually kind credit era. | Slip yourself into a list of peers as one of the lucky ones, then make it the reader's warning. |
| 124 | Bill Gross, "Cult Figures" (Aug 2012), excerpt https://www.marketfolly.com/2012/08/bill-gross-on-death-of-equities-pimco.html | "The cult of equity is dying." | "Unfair though it may be, an investor should continue to expect an attempted inflationary solution" | **A great five-word opening attached to a wrong call** (the S&P 500 has risen several-fold since). Voice without humility ages badly; Caplet's claims must be ones it can stand behind in 2033. |
| 125 | BlackRock Investment Institute, 2026 outlook, as reported https://www.investmentofficer.be/fr/analyse/document/22405 | - | "Mega forces, led by AI, are now the primary drivers of growth and returns" | **Counter-example:** stacked abstract nouns, coined labels. One label ("mega forces") is memorable; the rest is plastic at institutional scale. |
| 126 | Mike Wilson (Morgan Stanley) on missed forecasts, as reported https://www.benzinga.com/general/24/05/38679398/morgan-stanley-cio-cites-humbling-business-behind-wrong-forecasts-gives-up-predicting-s-p-500-as-one | - | Forecasting is "a humbling business." | Admit the field is hard, then move to where your edge is. Caplet's version: we don't forecast rates; we buy the dates. |

### 127-133 · Asness and Damodaran, more · reader
| # | Piece | Open | Best line | Steal |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| 127 | Asness, "I Did Not Predict What Is Going on in Privates" https://www.aqr.com/Insights/Perspectives/I-Did-Not-Predict-What-is-Going-on-in-Privates | "Privates are having a tough time lately." | "The lion doesn't care if the ostrich is first loss or higher up in the capital structure." | Refuse the credit you're offered ("I did not predict this"), then restate your narrower, true claim. |
| 128 | Asness, "Rebuffed: A Closer Look at Options-Based Strategies" https://www.aqr.com/Insights/Perspectives/Rebuffed-A-Closer-Look-at-Options-Based-Strategies | "The holy grail for many investors is a strategy that generates market-like returns, but with less risk." | "I'm not the only one at AQR who gets worked up about silliness in the industry." | "Nope." as a one-word paragraph after a hopeful question. **Insight:** buffered products sold on protection mostly lose to a cheap stocks-plus-cash mix. Confirms Caplet's "built at home" line (SYNTHESIS 7.3). |
| 129 | Asness, "In Praise of High-Volatility Alternatives" https://www.aqr.com/Insights/Perspectives/In-Praise-of-High-Volatility-Alternatives | "Everyone knows that it's compound returns over time that matter." | "I will never agree that I was wrong on the math." | A tiny numerical example before the complicated version. |
| 130 | Damodaran, "Data Update 4 for 2025: Interest Rates, Inflation and Central Banks!" https://aswathdamodaran.substack.com/p/data-update-4-for-2025-interest-rates | "It was an interesting year for interest rates in the United States" | "I must confess that I find the bond market far less interesting" | Admit your bias against your subject, then be fair to it. Insight: long yields rose in 2024 despite three Fed cuts. |
| 131 | Damodaran, "The Greed and Fear Tango: Markets in April 2025" https://aswathdamodaran.substack.com/p/the-greed-and-fear-tango-markets | "I started the month on a trip to Latin America, just as the tariff story hit my newsfeed" | "As someone who stinks at market timing, I made no attempt to buy and sell the market through the month" | Ends on a granddaughter's arrival, above the portfolio. Warmth by putting money second, once. |
| 132 | Damodaran, "A Golden Year (2025)" https://aswathdamodaran.substack.com/p/a-golden-year-2025-golds-price-surge | "I grew up in India in a time where if you had wealth, your investment options were limited." | "I must confess that I have never felt the draw of gold, and have never held it in my portfolio." | Sort the subject into categories first; the verdict then follows almost by deduction. |
| 133 | Damodaran, "Trillion Dollar Market Caps" https://aswathdamodaran.substack.com/p/trillion-dollar-market-caps-fairy | "Stock markets have always rewarded winners with large capitalizations" | "I will try to thread the needle (unsuccessfully, I am sure) in this post." | Turn a shouting match into a threshold: what must be true for this price? Then test each condition. |

Pattern: Damodaran opens four of seven posts with *where he was* (a beach, Latin America, India as a boy). The
personal first line is his signature, and it never lasts more than a sentence before the numbers start.

### 134-137 · Speeches: the strand on feeling, continued · reader
| # | Piece | Open | Turn | Close | How it moves | Steal |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 134 | Winston Churchill, "Blood, toil, tears and sweat" (1940) https://historyplace.com/speeches/churchill.htm | "I have nothing to offer but blood, toil, tears and sweat." | "...for without victory, there is no survival." | "Come then, let us go forward together with our united strength." | The price comes first, before any ask. Short, plain words; a drumbeat of repetition. | **Name the cost before the promise.** The strongest version of F3. |
| 135 | Barack Obama, DNC keynote (2004) https://billofrightsinstitute.org/activities/barack-obama-keynote-address-at-the-democratic-national-convention-july-27-2004/ | "let's face it, my presence on this stage is pretty unlikely." | "...the hope of a skinny kid with a funny name who believes that America has a place for him, too." | "...out of this long political darkness a brighter day will come." | Named people (a father herding goats, a grandmother on a bomber line); "hope" redefined as substance, not "blind optimism". | Abstract values carried by named people; a parallel list that ends on the speaker. |
| 136 | Lou Gehrig, farewell (1939) https://fordham.edu/Halsall/mod/1939gehrig-farewell.asp | "Fans, for the past two weeks you have been reading about a bad break I got." | (the "luckiest man" line) | "I have an awful lot to live for!" | A dying man calls it "a bad break" and spends the speech thanking people. Never names the fear; the gratitude stacks until the illness looks small. | **Understatement of the loss** moves more than description of it. |
| 137 | Randy Pausch, "The Last Lecture" (2007), via NPR https://www.npr.org/transcripts/92960436 | "I'm dying," he told them. "And yet I'm having fun." | "The brick walls are there to give us a chance to show how badly we want something." | - | The hardest fact in three words, then lightness. Grief and a smile in one breath. | State the worst fact plainly, then pivot at once to what the listener can do. |

The speech strand now says one thing loudly: **the speakers people remember say the bad news first and smallest**
("a bad break", "I'm dying", "nothing to offer but blood"). Caplet's version for the IPS: say the risk first, in its
real size ("the one thing that can break this plan is the United States not paying its debts"), then the plan.

Keel's plastic prose is exactly being "smoothed into the surroundings". |

**Count check on the strand (25 pieces):** the most-used emotional move is **the honest cost first** (Churchill, Gehrig,
Pausch, FDR, Kennedy, Chouinard, Google, Bezos 1997), then **named people** (Obama, King, Malala, Buffett on Munger).
Guide section 7 has both (F3, F2); synthesis 2 will rank them.

The title asks the question better than the abstract answers it. | A great question as a title can carry a dull abstract; Caplet has no such luxury in 500 words. The verdict goes first. |

doesn't earn income, it delivers outcomes on dates.

|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 205 | "Putting Ideas into Words" https://www.paulgraham.com/words.html | "Writing about something, even something you know well, usually shows you that you didn't know it as well as you thought." | "Ideas can feel complete. It's only when you try to put them into words that you discover they're not." | "But though it's not a sufficient condition, it is a necessary one." | Reread as a neutral stranger would. |
| 206 | "Writing, Briefly" https://www.paulgraham.com/writing44.html | - | "be confident enough to cut" | "learn to recognize the approach of an ending, and when one appears, grab it." | Rules for guide s9: "use simple, germanic words"; "don't try to sound impressive". |
| 207 | "Good Writing" https://www.paulgraham.com/goodwriting.html | "There are two senses in which writing can be good: it can sound good, and the ideas can be right." | "The sound of writing turns out to be more like the shape of a plane than the color of a car." | "It's hard to be right without sounding right." | When a passage sounds wrong, the idea behind it often is. Supports Ahaan's "judge by ear". |
| 208 | "How to Write Usefully" https://www.paulgraham.com/useful.html | "What should an essay be?" | "Useful writing is bold, but true." | "If most essays are still unwritten, most such ideas are still undiscovered." | Claims as strong as they can be without becoming false; qualification is a skill, not a hedge. Bible rule 6 in five words. |
| 209 | "Write Like You Talk" https://www.paulgraham.com/talk.html | "Here's a simple trick for getting more people to read what you write: write in spoken language." | "Informal language is the athletic clothing of ideas." | "...just don't let a sentence through unless it's the way you'd say it to a friend." | The friend test, now in guide s9. |
| 210 | "How to Convince Investors" https://www.paulgraham.com/convince.html | "When people hurt themselves lifting heavy things, it's usually because they try to lift with their back." | "They're far better at detecting bullshit than you are at producing it, even if you're producing it unknowingly." | "Make the truth good, then just tell it." | **For a Shark Tank judge:** formidable = justifiably confident; investors must be able to retell your case to colleagues. |
| 211 | "How to Present to Investors" https://www.paulgraham.com/investors.html | "In a few days it will be Demo Day..." | "Better a narrow description than a vague one." | "If you can't, your plans may not be sufficiently focused." | "Explain what you're doing" in the first sentence; "Specific numbers are good"; "Make a soundbite stick in their heads." |
| 212 | "The Best Essay" https://www.paulgraham.com/best.html | "Despite its title this isn't meant to be the best essay." | "Writing converts your ideas from vague to bad." | "That is the most important question of all." | Branches that don't pay off get cut, however long they took. |
| 213 | "Keep Your Identity Small" https://www.paulgraham.com/identity.html | "I finally realized today why politics and religion yield such uniquely useless discussions." | "But on religion everyone's an expert." | "The more labels you have for yourself, the dumber they make you." | Short essay, one idea, a parallel last line. |
| 214 | "Earnestness" https://www.paulgraham.com/earnest.html | "Jessica and I have certain words that have special significance when we're talking about startups." | "Caring constrains you." | "...an example of an x nerd in the wild." | Earnest beats cynical; three-word best line. |

| 215 | "How to Think for Yourself" https://www.paulgraham.com/think.html | "There are some kinds of work that you can't do well without thinking differently from your peers." | "The best place to find undiscovered ideas is where no one else is looking." | "...'do what you're curious about.'" | Replace a slogan the reader accepts with a sharper one. |
| 216 | "The Right Kind of Stubborn" https://www.paulgraham.com/persistence.html | "Successful people tend to be persistent." | "The persistent are attached to the goal. The obstinate are attached to their ideas about how to reach it." | "...the results are magical." | **A test for Caplet's own rule** (FRB-31): is "never sell a rung" a goal or a route? |
| 217 | "Is It Worth Being Wise?" https://www.paulgraham.com/wisdom.html | "A few days ago I finally figured out something I've wondered about for 25 years" | "The path to wisdom is through discipline, and the path to intelligence through carefully selected self-indulgence." | "Maybe you're just running fast." | Wisdom is average performance, intelligence the peaks: Caplet's cheques want wisdom, its gift wants a little intelligence. |
| 218 | "Heresy" https://www.paulgraham.com/heresy.html | "One of the most surprising things I've witnessed in my lifetime is the rebirth of the concept of heresy." | "It's like dropping a chunk of uranium onto the scale." | "...new tactics for resisting the next one." | Judge a worry by its direction of change, not its level. |
| 219 | "Beyond Smart" https://www.paulgraham.com/smart.html | "If you asked people what was special about Einstein, most would say that he was really smart." | "...a sort of wasteland that we try to hurry through with our eyes averted." | "...discoveries about discovery." | Turn a discouraging gap into a thing to study. |
| 220 | "What I Worked On" https://www.paulgraham.com/worked.html | "Before college the two main things I worked on, outside of school, were writing and programming." | "If anything is going to lead you astray, it will be the desire to impress people." | "...and this is the last sentence of it." | "I hereby apologize to my boss and coworkers, because I was a bad employee." **F6.** A self-aware last line. |
| 221 | "How Not to Die" https://www.paulgraham.com/die.html | "A couple days ago I told a reporter that we expected about a third of the companies we funded to succeed." | "Startups rarely die in mid keystroke. So keep typing!" | "Don't give up." | Three-word close after 2,000 words that all point at it. |
| 222 | "Having Kids" https://www.paulgraham.com/kids.html | "Before I had kids, I was afraid of having kids." | "Work, like love, finds a way." | "...so must the happiness that kids bring." | **Feeling without syrup:** he undercuts his own warmth (maybe it's chemical; he sounds like a cult member). **F5, F6.** |
| 223 | "Default Alive or Default Dead?" https://www.paulgraham.com/aord.html | "When I talk to a startup that's been operating for more than 8 or 9 months," | "Half the founders I talk to don't know whether they're default alive or default dead." | "...the astonishingly successful organism it is now." | **Turns a vague worry into a test anyone can run** (FRB-30). |
| 224 | "Six Principles for Making New Things" https://www.paulgraham.com/newthings.html | "The fiery reaction to the release of Arc had an unexpected consequence: it made me realize I had a design philosophy." | "...now it looks like they're merely floating downstream." | "But you ignore them because they look wrong." | A philosophy in six parallel phrases, explained after the list. |
| 225 | "The Bus Ticket Theory of Genius" https://www.paulgraham.com/genius.html | "Everyone knows that to do great work you need both natural ability and determination." | "...a disinterested obsession with something that matters." | "If that's the sort of thing you're interested in." | The "X theory of Y" title: a humble object against a grand idea. |
| 226 | "The Need to Read" https://www.paulgraham.com/read.html | "In the science fiction books I read as a kid..." | "You can't think well without writing well, and you can't write well without reading well." | "But people who want to have ideas can't afford to." | Long opening, balanced middle, short close: the three lengths in order. |

| 256 | 2022 https://www.midgardfinance.com/buffett/letters/2022/ | "Charlie Munger, my long-time partner, and I have the job of managing the savings of a great number of individuals." | "The weeds wither away in significance as the flowers bloom." | "We will have a good time and so will you." | **Opens on the reader's savings, not on Berkshire.** "Berkshire will always hold a boatload of cash and U.S. Treasury bills…" Coke's dividend: $75 million in 1994, $704 million by 2022. **F1.** |
| 257 | 2024 https://www.midgardfinance.com/buffett/letters/2024/ | "This letter comes to you as part of Berkshire's annual report." | "The cardinal sin is delaying the correction of mistakes" | "The Berkshire directors and I immensely enjoy having you come to Omaha…" | "Mistakes fade away; winners can forever blossom." A eulogy (Pete Liegl) inside an annual report. **F3.** |

FRB-36. |
| 262 | "A Number From Today and A Story About Tomorrow" https://collabfund.com/blog/numbersandstories/ | "Every forecast takes a number from today and multiplies it by a story about tomorrow." | "A fact multiplied by a story always equals something less than a fact." | "And people can come up with some wild stories." | **The find of the batch (FRB-32):** the opening sentence is the thesis, and the thesis is a formula. |
| 263 | "The Written Word" https://collabfund.com/blog/the-written-word/ | "A few thoughts I have about writing:" | "People don't remember what you wrote as much as they remember how they felt when they read it." | "Start with one brave sentence and see where it goes." | Ray's Shark Tank point in one line. |
| 264 | "Little Rules About Big Things" https://collabfund.com/blog/little-rules-about-big-things/ | "A few things I've come to terms with:" | "Most people can afford to not be a great investor. But they can't afford to be a bad investor." | "Everything is sales." | Two-word close after a list of truths. The "can't afford to be bad" line is Caplet's ladder in a sentence. |
| 265 | "Cumulative vs. Cyclical Knowledge" https://collabfund.com/blog/cumulative-vs-cyclical-knowledge/ | "President James Garfield died because the best doctors in the country didn't believe in germs…" | "The man who can buy anything he covets values nothing that he buys." | "I cannot ever imagine a world where economic volatility is tamed…" | **F4** (a death opens it). Money mistakes are cyclical: each generation relearns them. |

| 268 | "Black Swan Farming" https://www.paulgraham.com/swan.html | "I've done several types of work over the years but I don't know another as counterintuitive as startup investing." | "You need to do what you know intellectually to be right, even though it feels wrong." | "…if we want to fund more Airbnbs we have to stay good at thinking it." | The pilot in cloud trusting instruments over his body: an analogy for holding a plan through fear. |
| 269 | "Life is Short" https://www.paulgraham.com/vb.html | "Life is short, as everyone knows." | "The area under the curve is small, but its shape jabs into your consciousness like a pin." | "That's what you do when life is short." | **Turns time into a count** (weekends with a child). **F4.** Ten cheques are a count too. |

Closes that work end on a thing, not a lesson: "I stole his hiding place." (the cat). One coined verb carries a whole essay: "Sometimes I feel as though I "circle-live"". |

Most human line: "though our job is definitely not done." Human where it names who is hurt (lower-income households), flat where jargon goes unexplained. |

Batch 7 note: three of the best closes are three words or fewer ("Come by rail."; "See randomness."; "Everything is
sales."). Each lands because the piece has earned it. A short close with nothing behind it is a slogan. Housel's
#262 gives Caplet its cleanest idea so far: the promise is a fact; the gift is a fact times a story.

### 278-289 · Buffett, eleven more letters, and Abel's first (midgardfinance.com archive) · reader
| # | Letter | Open | Best line | Close | Craft · Insight |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 278 | 1977 https://www.midgardfinance.com/buffett/letters/1977/ | "Operating earnings in 1977 of $21,904,000, or $22.54 per share, were moderately better than anticipated a year ago." | "So much for single year snapshots as adequate portrayals of a business." | (an administrative note on Blue Chip Stamps) | Admits two years of wrong textile forecasts; the mills shrank from $53 million of net worth in 1955 to $22 million by 1964. **F3, F4.** Even Buffett's earliest letters end on paperwork. |
| 279 | 1984 https://www.midgardfinance.com/buffett/letters/1984/ | "Our gain in net worth during 1984 was $152.6 million, or $133 per share." | "bonds are as sound as a dollar - and we view the longterm outlook for dollars as dismal." | "We will have more to say about this investment in next year's report." | "I still find our errors embarrassing." **F3.** Inflation is the ladder's real enemy, which is why the gift half owns stocks (FRB-33). |
| 280 | 1987 https://www.midgardfinance.com/buffett/letters/1987/ | "Our gain in net worth during 1987 was $464 million, or 19.5%." | "Mr. Market is there to serve you, not to guide you." | "Mrs. B says you must beat last year's sales figures, and I have told her she won't be disappointed." | Ends on a person (Mrs. B), not a principle. Written after the October crash: volatility hurts only a forced seller. |
| 281 | 1990 https://www.midgardfinance.com/buffett/letters/1990/ | "Last year we made a prediction: "A reduction [in Berkshire's net worth] is almost certain in at least one of the next three years."" | "Charlie and I always have preferred a lumpy 15% return to a smooth 12%." | "I believe we would do very well financially; and I believe you would have just as much fun running the business…" | **Opens by quoting his own warning.** "Although I made an economic mistake in sticking with the textile business, I made no mistake in choosing Ken." **F3.** FRB-39. |
| 282 | 1992 https://www.midgardfinance.com/buffett/letters/1992/ | "Our per-share book value increased 20.3% during 1992." | "…buy good businesses at fair prices rather than fair businesses at good prices." | "I hope you will join us." | A bond's cash flows are printed on it; a stock's "coupons" must be estimated: the clearest line between Caplet's two halves. |
| 283 | 1994 https://www.midgardfinance.com/buffett/letters/1994/ | "Our gain in net worth during 1994 was $1.45 billion or 13.9%." | "Fear is the foe of the faddist, but the friend of the fundamentalist." | "This year it will be all smoke." | **A mistake measured to the cent:** USAir preferred bought for $358 million, written down to $89.5 million, "25 cents on the dollar", an "unforced error". **F3, F4.** |
| 284 | 1997 https://www.midgardfinance.com/buffett/letters/1997/ | "Our gain in net worth during 1997 was $8.0 billion…" | "Only those who will be sellers of equities in the near future should be happy at seeing stocks rise." | "Come to Omaha -- the cradle of capitalism -- in May and enjoy yourself." | Inverts what a reader is supposed to cheer for. Caplet buys its stock fund in 2028, so lower prices before then help Laura. |
| 285 | 1999 https://www.midgardfinance.com/buffett/letters/1999/ | "Our gain in net worth during 1999 was $358 million…" | "Buying dollar bills for $1.10 is not good business for those who stick around." | "Join us at the Capitalist Caper on Capitol Avenue." | **Grades himself:** his 1999 grade "most assuredly is a D". **F3, F6.** |
| 286 | 2003 https://www.midgardfinance.com/buffett/letters/2003/ | "Our gain in net worth during 2003 was $13.6 billion…" | "It's a painful condition to be in – but not as painful as doing something stupid." | "So join us at the Qwest for our annual Woodstock for Capitalists." | On sitting on idle cash. Caplet's version of patience: nothing traded between 2028 and 2033. |
| 287 | 2006 https://www.midgardfinance.com/buffett/letters/2006/ | "Our gain in net worth during 2006 was $16.9 billion…" | (the reader returned "Be fearful when others are greedy…", which Buffett first wrote in 1986; not confirmed in this letter) | "We'll see you there." | Credits luck (a calm year for insurance) for a record gain. **F6.** |
| 288 | 2013 https://www.midgardfinance.com/buffett/letters/2013/ | "Berkshire's gain in net worth during 2013 was $34.2 billion." | "Put 10% of the cash in short-term government bonds and 90% in a very low-cost S&P 500 index fund." | "In the meantime, come to Omaha on May 3rd and enjoy our Woodstock for Capitalists." | **His own instructions for his wife's trust** (F2: a promise with a name on it). FRB-38. |
| 289 | 2025, Greg Abel's first letter (28 Feb 2026) https://www.midgardfinance.com/buffett/letters/2025/ | "Warren Buffett is arguably the greatest investor of all time, with generations benefiting from his investment acumen." | "Your capital is commingled with ours, but it does not belong to us." | "We move forward with great intent and purpose." | "Warren is obviously a very hard act to follow." **F6.** A new voice keeping an old promise: the custodian's line is Caplet's line about Laura's money. Cash and Treasuries "now exceed $370 billion". |

### 299-308 · Paul Graham, ten more · reader

|---|---|---|---|---|---|

| 300 | "How to Work Hard" https://www.paulgraham.com/hwh.html | "It might not seem there's much to learn about how to work hard." | "Working hard is not just a dial you turn up to 11." | "But if you're consistently honest and clear-sighted, it will automatically assume an optimal shape…" | Opens by doubting its own premise. |
| 301 | "A Project of One's Own" https://www.paulgraham.com/own.html | "A few days ago, on the way home from school, my nine year old son told me he couldn't wait to get home…" | "Working on a project of your own is as different from ordinary work as skating is from walking." | "Ideally we can have the best of both worlds…" | **F1:** a child's sentence opens it; one metaphor carries the whole essay. |

| 306 | "Relentlessly Resourceful" https://www.paulgraham.com/relres.html | "A couple days ago I finally got being a good startup founder down to two words: relentlessly resourceful." | "To be hapless is to be battered by circumstances." | "'Make something people want' is the destination, but 'Be relentlessly resourceful' is how you get there." | **Defines a phrase by its opposite**, then tests it. ### 312 · E. B. White, "Death of a Pig" (1948), second-hand · reader
| # | Sources | What it shows |
|---|---|---|
| 312 | https://kottke.org/24/10/death-of-a-pig-by-eb-white-1948 and https://niemanstoryboard.org/?p=999 | Opens: "I spent several days and nights in mid-September with an ailing pig and I feel driven to account for this stretch of time,". "It is a tragedy enacted on most farms with perfect fidelity to the original script." "My pig simply failed to show up for a meal." Ends: "The loss we felt was not the loss of ham but the loss of pig." **Comedy first, so the grief lands** (the dachshund, the vet's fiancée, "Poor piggledy-wiggledy!"). The narrator's self-importance is the joke. **F5, F6.** |

Batch 8 note: Buffett's letters measure his mistakes to the cent (USAir at 25 cents on the dollar; a "D" for 1999;
the mills shrinking from $53 million to $22 million). A mistake given a number is believed; a mistake called a
"learning experience" is not.
