### 313-316 · Finance writing, good and generic · reader
| # | Piece | Open | Best (or most typical) line | Close | Note |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 313 | Evan Armstrong, "Vibes Are a Legitimate Economic Indicator" (Every, 2024) https://every.to/napkin-math/vibes-are-a-legitimate-economic-indicator | "Kyla Scanlon is good at the internet." | "I would characterize economic vibes as collective feelings in the digital age." | "…Vibes feel (ha!) like a bigger idea to me." | A seven-word opening about a person, not a topic. Laughs at his own pun in brackets. **F6.** |
| 314 | Columbia Threadneedle, "2025 fixed income outlook: a year of opportunity amid economic uncertainty" https://www.columbiathreadneedle.com/en/lu/intermediary/insights/2025-fixed-income-outlook-a-year-of-opportunity-amid-economic-uncertainty | "With inflation cooling and the US Federal Reserve transitioning from rate hikes to a cycle of rate cuts…" | "bonds are well-positioned to generate healthy returns" | "The tailwind from falling interest rates will only further boost total returns." | **Counter-example.** The title holds both "opportunity" and "uncertainty"; every verb is "should", "could" or "may"; no number says what would prove it wrong. Useful bit: default rates near 1% in some sectors and above 10% in others. |
| 315 | Cambridge Associates, "2025 Outlook: Interest Rates" https://www.cambridgeassociates.com/en-eu/insight/2025-outlook-interest-rates/ | "Moderating inflation and near-trend economic growth will allow most major central banks to bring policy rates toward neutral…" | "Given this uncertainty, we recommend a neutral allocation to high-quality bonds." | "…we believe linkers will generate solid returns in 2025, but we do not see a compelling case to overweight…" | **Counter-example**, though it gives its number (10-year at 4.2% against a fair value of 4.1%). A view that says "neutral" twice has no view. |
| 316 | "Overwhelmed by Noise" (The Pursuit of Compounding, Substack) https://thepursuitofcompounding.substack.com/p/overwhelmed-by-noise | "Spend anytime in the financial markets and a standard narrative of market efficiency emerges - it goes something like this:" | "Efficiency is the enemy of opportunity." | "Be a shark - there is more opportunity than ever." | **Counter-example.** Ends on a slogan with nothing behind it. "Be a shark" is what Caplet must not sound like in a Shark Tank. |

Net Interest (Rubinstein) note, for its opening only: "My portfolio manager always used to say that the devil has the best tunes." (https://substack.com/@netinterest/note/c-219601548)

### 317-322 · Essayists and newsletter writers · reader

|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 317 | Montaigne, "That to philosophize is to learn to die" (1580) https://www.hyperessays.net/pdf/essays/to-philosophize-is-to-learn-to-die-US.pdf | "Cicero says that to philosophize is nothing more than to prepare for death." | "Preparing for death is preparing for freedom." | "Happy is the death that gives this lot no time to prepare." | Opens on an authority, then tests it against his own life. | 318 | Hitchens, "Believe Me, It's Torture" (Vanity Fair, 2008), excerpts via https://reason.com/?p=127312 | (not given) | "Well, then, if waterboarding does not constitute torture, then there is no such thing as torture." | "I find I don't want to tell you how little time I lasted." | **Argues by doing the thing himself.** The close admits a cost he won't number: the withheld number is the most powerful one. **F3, F4 (inverted).** |
| 319 | Hitchens, *Mortality* (2012), via a review https://krabarchive.com/ralphmag/HD/hitchens.html | "Once you have heard it the wrong way," | "How happily I measured off my day as I saw the injection being readied." | "Brave? Hah! Save it for a fight you can't run away from." | Refuses the heroic frame in five words. **F6.** |
| 320 | Didion, "On Keeping a Notebook" (1966) https://www.newberry.org/uploads/files/OnKeepingaNotebook.pdf | "'That woman Estelle,'" the note reads, "'is partly the reason why George Sharp and I are separated today.'" | "Remember what it was to be me: that is always the point." | "I made the sauerkraut again last night and it did not make me feel any safer, but that is, as they say, another story." | **Opens on a found fragment and questions it.** Ends on sauerkraut, a thing, with a shrug. **F1, F6.** |
| 321 | Noah Smith, "Thoughts on techno-optimism" (Noahpinion) https://www.noahpinion.blog/p/thoughts-on-techno-optimism | "In my roundup this week, I endorsed Marc Andreessen's "Techno-Optimist Manifesto"." | "The enemy of techno-optimism isn't sustainability; it's *short-termism*." | "It's a faith in humanity — and all sentient beings — propelling ourselves forward into the infinite tomorrow." | Concedes where allies are wrong before he argues. The close swells too far (a counter-example in itself). |

"A defensible investment process still requires discipline through prolonged underperformance." |

### 324-339 · Buffett, sixteen more letters (midgardfinance.com archive) · reader
| # | Letter | Open | Best line | Close | Craft · Insight |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 324 | 1979 https://www.midgardfinance.com/buffett/letters/1979/ | "Again, we must lead off with a few words about accounting." | "You do not adequately protect yourself by being half awake while others are sleeping." | "We have placed much trust in them - and their achievements have far exceeded that trust." | **Admits holding fifteen-year bonds into inflation was a mistake.** An "8% U.S. Treasury Note" can "chew up, rather than enhance, purchasing power". **F3.** FRB-41. |
| 325 | 1980 https://www.midgardfinance.com/buffett/letters/1980/ | "Operating earnings improved to $41.9 million in 1980 from $36.0 million in 1979…" | "You may feel richer, but you won't eat richer." | "Whichever the relationship, it always was a special one, and I miss it." | **Ends on grief for a friend** (Gene Abegg), not on numbers. **F5.** |
| 326 | 1982 https://www.midgardfinance.com/buffett/letters/1982/ | "Operating earnings of $31.5 million in 1982 amounted to only 9.8% of beginning equity capital…" | "Under such circumstances, a marvelous business purchased at a fair sales price becomes a terrible buy." | "If we can continue to attract managers with the qualities of Ben and Phil, you need not worry about Berkshire's future." | Opens on the word "only": bad news in sentence one. **F3.** |
| 327 | 1985 https://www.midgardfinance.com/buffett/letters/1985/ | "You may remember the wildly upbeat message of last year's report: nothing much was in the works…" | "…a good managerial record (measured by economic returns) is far more a function of what business boat you get into…" | "And bring money: Mrs. B promises to have bargains galore if you will pay her a visit…" | **Opens with irony at his own expense.** Closes the textile mills: "should be faulted for not quitting sooner". **F3, F6.** |
| 328 | 1986 https://www.midgardfinance.com/buffett/letters/1986/ | "Our gain in net worth during 1986 was $492.5 million, or 26.1%." | "…we simply attempt to be fearful when others are greedy and to be greedy only when others are fearful." | "…accounting is but an aid to business thinking, never a substitute for it." | The original of the famous line (see #287). |
| 329 | 1988 https://www.midgardfinance.com/buffett/letters/1988/ | "Our gain in net worth during 1988 was $569 million, or 20.0%." | "…our favorite holding period is forever." | "I can sell so cheap 'cause I work for this dummy who doesn't know anything about carpet." | **Ends on someone else's joke at his expense.** **F6.** |
| 330 | 1989 https://www.midgardfinance.com/buffett/letters/1989/ | "Our gain in net worth during 1989 was $1.515 billion, or 44.4%." | "It's far better to buy a wonderful company at a fair price than a fair company at a wonderful price." | "If you can bear having your own rate turn negative for a day - not a pretty thought, I admit - visit Ike on the 29th." | Contains "Mistakes of the First Twenty-five Years": a whole section of errors. **F3.** |
| 331 | 1991 https://www.midgardfinance.com/buffett/letters/1991/ | "Our gain in net worth during 1991 was $2.1 billion, or 39.6%." | "In investing, just as in baseball, to put runs on the scoreboard one must watch the playing field, not the scoreboard." | "…so join us to watch a continuation of this lop-sided battle of wits." | Prices his Fannie Mae miss at roughly $1.4 billion (reader's figure; re-check). **F3, F4.** |
| 332 | 1993 https://www.midgardfinance.com/buffett/letters/1993/ | "Our per-share book value increased 14.3% during 1993." | "It is better to be approximately right than precisely wrong." | "I regret to report that you won't have to buy them from scalpers." | **Risk "using dictionary terms": "the possibility of loss or injury"**, not volatility. "the knownothing investor can actually out-perform most investment professionals." FRB-43. |
| 333 | 1995 https://www.midgardfinance.com/buffett/letters/1995/ | "Our gain in net worth during 1995 was $5.3 billion, or 45.0%." | "A bad reinsurance contract is like hell: easy to enter and impossible to exit." | "You'll spot me there - I'll be the one eating the rare T-bone with a double order of hash browns." | Thanks the man who gave him a free half-day lesson in 1951. Prices his Gillette choice at ~$625 million (reader's figure). |
| 334 | 1998 https://www.midgardfinance.com/buffett/letters/1998/ | "Our gain in net worth during 1998 was $25.9 billion…" | (the reader returned the "lumpy 15%" line from 1990; not confirmed here) | "To all of the 11.8, my thanks." | **A four-word close with a number in it** (his 11.8-person head office). |
| 335 | 2004 https://www.midgardfinance.com/buffett/letters/2004/ | "Our gain in net worth during 2004 was $8.3 billion…" | "Investors should remember that excitement and expenses are their enemies." | "So join us on April 30th at the Qwest for our annual Woodstock for Capitalists." | Admits failing to act on his own bubble-era worries. **F3.** FRB-29. |
| 336 | 2015 https://www.midgardfinance.com/buffett/letters/2015/ | "Berkshire's gain in net worth during 2015 was $15.4 billion…" | "For 240 years it's been a terrible mistake to bet against America, and now is no time to start." | (not reached: the page was cut off) | Today's children "the luckiest crop in history". |
| 337 | 2017 https://www.midgardfinance.com/buffett/letters/2017/ | "Berkshire's gain in net worth during 2017 was $65.3 billion…" | "Performance comes, performance goes. Fees never falter." | "All of us look forward to your visit." | The hedge-fund bet: the charity got $2,222,279. Over long horizons stocks can be less risky than bonds; over short ones far riskier (FRB-43). **F4.** |
| 338 | 2019 https://www.midgardfinance.com/buffett/letters/2019/ | "Berkshire earned $81.4 billion in 2019 according to generally accepted accounting principles…" | "When seeking directors, CEOs don't look for pit bulls. It's the cocker spaniel that gets taken home." | "Charlie and I – along with the entire Berkshire gang – are looking forward to seeing you." | Tells readers to ignore a $53.7 billion swing in unrealised gains. |
| 339 | 2021 https://www.midgardfinance.com/buffett/letters/2021/ | "Charlie Munger, my long-time partner, and I have the job of managing a portion of your savings." | "Charlie and I are not stock-pickers; we are business-pickers." | "Join me." | **Two-word close.** Opens on the reader's savings (as 2022, #256). |

Batch 9 note: the generic outlooks (314-316) fail in the same three ways: every verb is a maybe, no number could prove
them wrong, and the close is a slogan. Hitchens (318) and Buffett (324) do the opposite: each tells the reader what
it cost him. That is the gap between commentary and a plan.

Batch 10 note: the six Hopkins essays split three and three. The three that end on an image or a callback (354, 355,
359) are the ones whose last line you can still picture an hour later; the three that end on a lesson (356-358) end
on the weakest sentence they contain. Synthesis 3's rule holds. Grantham's best piece (340) was written in the week
of the 2009 low and is a plan, not a forecast.

| 376 | "How to Get New Ideas" https://www.paulgraham.com/getideas.html | "The way to get new ideas is to notice anomalies: what seems strange, or missing, or broken?" | "Knowledge grows fractally." | "In the best case, exploring such gaps yields whole new fractal buds." | A 100-word essay: one idea, one image. |

| 378 | "Is There Such a Thing as Good Taste?" https://www.paulgraham.com/goodtaste.html | "When I was a kid, I'd have said there wasn't." | "That gap between us was not imaginary." | "So while it's not possible to have perfect taste, it is possible to have good taste." | **Answers his own title with his younger self's wrong answer.** **F6.** |
| 379 | "Fierce Nerds" https://www.paulgraham.com/fn.html | "Most people think of nerds as quiet, diffident people." | "If you're given a sharp knife, it seems to me better to use it than to blunt its edge to avoid cutting yourself." | "At least not till the nerds end it themselves by bringing about the singularity." | Opens on what "most people think", then breaks it. |
| 380 | "Crazy New Ideas" https://www.paulgraham.com/newideas.html | "There's one kind of opinion I'd be very afraid to express publicly." | "It doesn't just affect the way we think; it is the Lego blocks we build thoughts out of." | "And if you help them, you'll probably learn something in the process." | "Few understand how feeble new ideas look when they first appear." |
| 381 | "The Two Kinds of Moderate" https://www.paulgraham.com/mod.html | "There are two distinct ways to be politically moderate: on purpose and by accident." | "Nearly all the most impressive people I know are accidental moderates." | "…you have two choices…" | Defines by a pair. |
| 382 | "How You Know" https://www.paulgraham.com/know.html | "I've read Villehardouin's chronicle of the Fourth Crusade at least two times, maybe three." | "Your mind is like a compiled program you've lost the source of." | "So although not knowing how you know things may seem part of being human, it may not be." | **The opening admits he can't remember**, which is the essay's subject. |
| 383 | "Mean People Fail" https://www.paulgraham.com/mean.html | "It struck me recently how few of the most successful people I know are mean." | "Startups don't win by attacking. They win by transcending." | "…because living at all is a scarce resource." | Two short sentences, then the turn. |
| 384 | "Maker's Schedule, Manager's Schedule" https://www.paulgraham.com/makersschedule.html | "One reason programmers dislike meetings so much is that they're on a different type of schedule from other people." | "For someone on the maker's schedule, having a meeting is like throwing an exception." | "All we ask from those on the manager's schedule is that they understand the cost." | **The close asks for one thing, and names a cost.** **F3.** |
| 385 | "Good and Bad Procrastination" https://www.paulgraham.com/procrastination.html | "The most impressive people I know are all terrible procrastinators." | "It's like having a vacuum cleaner hooked up to your imagination." | "…you'll leave the right things undone." | Opens on a paradox. |
| 386 | "Some Heroes" https://www.paulgraham.com/heroes.html | "There are some topics I save up because they'll be so much fun to write about." | "They just sit there quietly radiating optimism, like a battery that never runs out." | "If we work like Newton." | Praise made specific (Clark's prose like a sports car). |
| 387 | "Mind the Gap" https://www.paulgraham.com/gap.html | "When people care enough about something to do it well, those who do it best tend to be far better than everyone else." | "I'm saying that he'll make you a tractor to replace your horse." | "Perhaps the designers of admissions processes should take a lesson from the example of computer security…" | "Where does wealth come from? People make it." Wealth made, not split: why the gift half owns companies. |
| 388 | "Be Good" https://www.paulgraham.com/good.html | "About a month after we started Y Combinator we came up with the phrase that became our motto: Make something people want." | "You grow big by being nice, but you can stay big by being mean." | "Be good." | **Two-word close** that the title already promised. |
| 389 | "You Weren't Meant to Have a Boss" https://www.paulgraham.com/boss.html | "Technology tends to separate normal from natural." | "In an artificial world, only extremists live naturally." | "…finally they're working the way…" | One food metaphor carried through. |
| 390 | "Do Things that Don't Scale" https://www.paulgraham.com/ds.html | "One of the most common types of advice we give at Y Combinator is to do things that don't scale." | "I have never once seen a startup lured down a blind alley by trying too hard to make their initial users happy." | "…if you have to work hard to delight users when you only have a handful of them, …" | "I have never once seen": certainty from experience, no hedge. |

How can you beat five players by running in a straight line?" "The answer is that the English defenders reacted to what they expected Maradona to do." **A governor explains expectations with a goal everyone has seen.** FRB-49. |

Batch 11 note: Graham's best closes are two words because his titles already made the promise ("Be good.", "See
randomness."). The title and the last line are a pair. Caplet's IPS has no title, but the pitch and the IPS's last
line can be that pair.

### 415-417 · Damodaran on what "risk-free" means · blog
| # | Piece | Open | Best line | Close | Craft · Insight |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 415 | Damodaran, "What if nothing is risk free?" (2024) https://aswathdamodaran.substack.com/p/what-if-nothing-is-risk-free-10-07-24 | "Every discipline develops its own dogma and finance is no exception" | "Those within the discipline either take these fundamental assumptions as given or are reluctant to question them." | "When firms have no risk free investments, lenders to these firms will be more wary about lending to them" | Opens by putting his own field on trial. **F6.** The risk-free asset is an assumption, not a law. |
| 416 | Damodaran, "Chill, dude! Part II: debt default drama" (2013) https://aswathdamodaran.substack.com/p/chill-dude-part-ii-debt-default-drama-13-10-16 | "When I took my first finance class, I was taught that the government bond rate in the currency in question…" | "it is too late to put the genie back in the bottle" | "The good news is that the mechanism for incorporating that shift into valuation and corporate finance exists,…" | A slangy title over dry prose: the joke is in the title, never in the sums. US CDS spread 0.105% (Jan 2008) to 0.73% (Jan 2009): default risk is a matter of degree. **F4.** |
| 417 | Damodaran, "What is the riskfree rate?" (2020) https://aswathdamodaran.substack.com/p/what-is-the-risk-free-rate-08-09-20 | "The risk free rate is the building block on which we erect risk premiums." | "The implicit assumption, not often stated, was that the US Treasury was incapable of default." | "It may be time to rewrite finance textbooks to reflect the new realities." | The first sentence is the thesis, as plain as a definition. The close tells the textbook to change, not the reader. |

### 429-440 · The RBA, 2024-25: Hauser, Hunter, Bullock · speeches
| # | Speech | Open | Best line | Close | Craft · Insight |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 429 | Hauser, "The Ghost of Christmas Yet to Come" (Dec 2024) https://www.rba.gov.au/speeches/2024/sp-dg-2024-12-11.html | "It is a pleasure to be here." | "while my former colleagues at the Bank of England scrape frost off windscreens and thaw frozen pipes" | "I wish you all a very happy festive season, and look forward to our discussion this evening." | Scrooge's arc carries a trade-war outlook: the feared future, then the one that is still possible. A whole frame, used once, for the season. |
| 430 | Hauser, "Beware False Prophets" (Aug 2024) https://www.rba.gov.au/speeches/2024/sp-dg-2024-08-12.html | "My theme today is learning and the role it plays in monetary policymaking." | "In short, it's a world of winners and losers, gurus and charlatans, geniuses and buffoons." | "But beware anyone who claims it is obvious what to do – for they are false prophets!" | Point forecasts are nearly always wrong, and the bands around them are too narrow because they ignore that the model itself may be wrong. **The close repeats the title.** |
| 431 | Hauser, "Strangers in Paradise" (Jun 2024) https://www.rba.gov.au/speeches/2024/sp-dg-2024-06-27.html | "Picture if you can a thriving trading hub on the Australian coast." | "it would be brave for a new and infatuated stranger to declare Australia an earthly paradise." | "I wish you well in those discussions." | **F1** in the first line. The newcomer laughs at his own enthusiasm. |
| 432 | Hauser, "From the Shadows to the Podium" (Oct 2024) https://www.rba.gov.au/speeches/2024/sp-dg-2024-10-08.html | "It's a privilege to be with you today and to announce the shortlist for the 2024 Walkley Business Journalism Award." | "Poor arguments, which once went unquestioned in grey smoke-filled rooms, did not survive the rigour of public examination." | "Thank you." | "So communications is everything – or almost everything." The correction inside the sentence is the voice. |
| 433 | Hunter, "Shedding Light on Uncertainty: Using Scenarios in Forecasting and Policy" (Dec 2024) https://www.rba.gov.au/speeches/2024/sp-ag-2024-12-13.html | (acknowledgement of Country) | "But it's only one path, and the chance of that precise path being the one that happens is approximately zero." | "We hope the scenarios we publish help financial markets and the public better understand the forecasts…" | A dry "approximately zero". 90% of the RBA's two-year inflation forecasts since 1993 landed within ±2 points: a range with a stated hit rate. **F4.** |
| 434 | Bullock, "The Costs of High Inflation" (Sep 2024) https://www.rba.gov.au/speeches/2024/sp-gov-2024-09-05.html | "I am very pleased to be here at the Anika Foundation fundraising luncheon." | "But inflation causes hardship too, for all Australians and particularly for the more vulnerable in our community." | "…our full employment goal is not served by letting inflation stay above target indefinitely." | Plain, warm, "we" and "I". Says who is hurt before saying what the bank will do. |
| 435 | Hauser, "A Hedge Between Keeps Friendship Green" (Sep 2025) https://www.rba.gov.au/speeches/2025/sp-dg-2025-09-16.html | "It is a privilege to be invited to address the Board of CLS – or 'Continuous Linked Settlement' in longhand –…" | "predictions of the death of the US dollar and the Australian hedging model appear somewhat premature" | "…we look forward to continuing to do all we can to help in that endeavour." | A proverb as a title for currency hedging, then a Twain echo ("somewhat premature"). If the home currency falls when markets fall, it hedges for free; if the dollar stops being a haven, that changes. |
| 436 | Hauser, "Monetary Policy in a VUCA World" (Mar 2025) https://www.rba.gov.au/speeches/2025/sp-dg-2025-03-05.html | "In the late 1980s, as the Iron Curtain fell, the US Army War College threw away its old Cold War playbook." | "Central bankers are paid to worry, not celebrate." | "…leaving them free to focus on navigating an increasingly VUCA world." | **F1** with a date and a place. Prepare for many outcomes, choose robust decisions, say the uncertainty out loud. |
| 437 | Hauser, "On the Rail or Off to the Races?" (Nov 2025) https://www.rba.gov.au/speeches/2025/sp-dg-2025-11-10.html | "It's great to be here with you today, to speak with, and hear from, investors from Australia and around the world." | "If that doesn't scream 'investment potential', I don't know what does." | "I look forward to answering your questions today." | Melbourne Cup week: three race "tracks" and a horse called Reserve Bank. "If we fail to do so, we may find ourselves boxed in on the rail. If we succeed, we could be off to the races." A local frame used once and fully. |
| 438 | Hunter, "Monetary Policy: Forward Looking and Data Dependent in the Face of Uncertainty" (Mar 2025) https://www.rba.gov.au/speeches/2025/sp-ag-2025-03-18.html | "Three weeks ago, the Reserve Bank Board cut interest rates for the first time since 2020." | "we don't have a crystal ball and even if we did it would be very cloudy." | "This is why being forward looking is not in tension with being data dependent." | A cliché turned at the end ("very cloudy"). Not jerked about by every data point. |
| 439 | Bullock, "The RBA's Dual Mandate – Inflation and Employment" (Jul 2025) https://www.rba.gov.au/speeches/2025/sp-gov-2025-07-24.html | "It's an honour to join you today at the Anika Foundation fundraising lunch." | "Having a strong track record of low and stable inflation puts us in the best possible position to support employment." | "And that is what the Board is constantly striving for." | A list of four "good for"s. Plain to the point of flat: a useful control sample. |
| 440 | Hauser, "What Has Australian Macroeconomic Thought Achieved in the Past Century…" (Jul 2025) https://www.rba.gov.au/speeches/2025/sp-dg-2025-07-09.html | "It is a great honour to address you on the 100th anniversary of the Economics Society of Australia." | "Giblin was a model Accidental Economist." | "Thank you." | History told as characters. Short labels ("Two Golden Ages") carry the structure. |

### 441-445 · Orwell, Montaigne; two more RBA · reader
| # | Piece | What it shows |
|---|---|---|
| 441 | Orwell, "Bookshop Memories" (1936) https://fortnightlyreview.co.uk/?p=9924 | Open: "WHEN I WORKED in a second-hand bookshop—so easily pictured, if you don't work in one, as a kind of paradise…" Best: "A bookseller has to tell lies about books, and that gives him a distaste for them." Close: "It is too closely associated in my mind with paranoiac customers and dead bluebottles." **The close is the oddest concrete detail in the piece.** |
| 442 | Montaigne, "Of Experience" (1588, Cotton tr.) https://www.gutenberg.org/cache/epub/3599/pg3599-images.html (first 100,000 characters read) | Open: "There is no desire more natural than that of knowledge." "Men do not know the natural disease of the mind; it does nothing but ferret and inquire". Writes about his own illnesses and habits to reach general truths. **F6.** |
| 443 | Hauser, "Open the Door and See the Mountain" (May 2025) https://www.rba.gov.au/speeches/2025/sp-dg-2025-05-22.html | Opens on a Mandarin proverb and idle cranes on the skyline; "don't count China out." "Some of the judgements may prove wide of the mark". Travel writing that admits its limits. |
| 444 | Hunter, "Joining the Dots" (Jun 2025) https://www.rba.gov.au/speeches/2025/sp-ag-2025-06-03.html | "The short answer is we can't be completely sure." Then explains the channels anyway. A control sample: measured, careful, and a little grey. |
| 445 | Kyla Scanlon on Macro Musings (Mercatus transcript) https://www.mercatus.org/macro-musings/kyla-scanlon-vibecession-vibe-economy-and-path-growing-american-wealth | First answer: "Oh, thanks for having me." "Constraining supply is the best way to kill a city." "Should a house be both a speculative asset and a place that you live? Probably not." The question, then a two-word answer. |

Batch 13 note: the RBA under Hauser writes the way the best investors do. One frame per speech (Scrooge, the
Melbourne Cup, a War College playbook), used once and fully, then dropped. Every speech says out loud that a single
forecast is almost surely wrong (430, 433, 436, 438). The plain ones (439, 444) are good to learn from by
contrast: correct, warm and forgettable. The jokes sit in titles and first lines; the numbers are left alone.

Says his forecast is worthless, then commits to a policy anyway. **F6 then F2.** |

"The inflation genie is out of the bottle" (a phrase he reuses, see 416). |

A short promise, no apology. |

(last line of the excerpt) | **F1** by accumulation: the body, the clock, the interruptions, then the confession: "And the reviewer is an utterly shameless hack." |

| 465 | "Left and Right Don't Exist" (2025) https://apply.jhu.edu/hopkins-insider/left-and-right-dont-exist/ | "The first lesson I learned as a student pilot is that left and right don't exist." | "But at 36,000 feet in the air in a steel tube hurdling almost 200 miles an hour?" | "Whether I was 36,000 feet in the air or on ground level, by flying and writing, I realized that the most…" | A first line that sounds false and is true. **F1.** |
| 466 | "Queen's Gambit" (2025) https://apply.jhu.edu/hopkins-insider/queens-gambit/ | "I was thoroughly confused." | "Through chess and soccer, I have discovered that every piece in a puzzle has a purpose." | "Your move." | **Two-word close that hands the reader the next turn.** |

A rule of solitaire becomes a rule for life. |
| 468 | "Stepping Out of My Comfort Zone" (2025) https://apply.jhu.edu/hopkins-insider/stepping-out-of-my-comfort-zone/ | "If you told me I would be playing a sport called squash at 11 years old, I would call you crazy." | "I felt like a little fish in a big pond." | "…and I can't wait to be a part of the Hopkins family." | A cliché title and a cliché image; it worked on the story, not the prose. |

| 471 | "Switching Shoes" (2024) https://apply.jhu.edu/hopkins-insider/switching-shoes/ | "It was a wet and dreary October evening." | "Click, clack, click. The sound echoed through my head" | "Rather, I pour my heart into such opportunities and take their experiences with me." | A sound repeated as a refrain. |

| 473 | "Translating My Story Into Words" (2024) https://apply.jhu.edu/hopkins-insider/translating-my-story-into-words/ | "My eyes widen." | "That day, I translated every note into a jump until my body understood the music." | "…I strive to learn and translate my knowledge into action that creates change." | Three-word open; the title's verb comes back in the best line. |

Batch 14 note: the RBA's people are most human off-script (451, 453, 454, 456, 457): a guitar, a cold from a
granddaughter, a joke about raves. Their prepared speeches (448, 450) are correct and grey. The Hopkins essays show the
same split inside one page: strong first lines ("I cannot dance.", "My eyes widen.") and weak, generic last lines. The
closes that work (466 "Your move.", 463 "Therefore farewell.") are short and give the reader something to do or feel.

### 474-476 · Three old essayists · essays
| # | Essay | Open | Best line | Close | Craft |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 474 | Hazlitt, "On Going a Journey" (1822) https://sites.ualberta.ca/~dmiall/Travel/hazlitt.htm | "One of the pleasantest things in the world is going a journey; but I like to go by myself." | "I am then never less alone than when alone." | "…if I could anywhere borrow another life to spend afterwards at home!" | **Grants the common view and turns on "but" in the first sentence.** The paradox line is the one people quote. |
| 475 | Lamb, "Dream-Children: A Reverie" (1822) https://essays.quotidiana.org/lamb/dream_children_a_reverie/ | "Children love to listen to stories about their elders, when they were children; …" | "We are nothing; less than nothing, and dreams." | "…but John L. (or James Elia) was gone forever." | **F5 reversed: warmth, then the floor drops.** The children he describes were never born; the turn comes in one line. "I missed his kindness, and I missed his crossness". |
| 476 | Woolf, "The Death of the Moth" (1942) https://gutenberg.net.au/ebooks12/1203811h.html | "Moths that fly by day are not properly to be called moths;" | "He was little or nothing but life." | "O yes, he seemed to say, death is stronger than I am." | One insect on a windowsill carries the whole essay. The smallest possible subject, watched closely. **F1.** |

### 477-483 · Marks; Haldane, five speeches; Orwell's toads · reader
| # | Piece | Open | Best line | Close | Craft · Insight |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 477 | Marks, "Whad'ya Know?" (2003) https://www.oaktreecapital.com/docs/default-source/memos/2003-03-11-whad-ya-know.pdf | "I always ask Nancy to read my memos before I send them out." | "The concept of accurate ratings is dead; long live the rating agencies!" | "Or perhaps Blondie's take was the most profound:" | **F6 in line one** (his wife as editor). The future can't be known, so avoid the bets where a wrong call is ruinous. |
| 478 | Haldane, "Thirty years of hurt, never stopped me dreaming" (2021) https://bis.org/review/r210702d.htm | "At the end of September I leave the Bank of England, 32 years almost to the day since I joined." | "In public policy, crises are the ultimate learning experience." | "I am very fortunate to have experienced plenty of both." | A football chant as the title of a farewell. The first sentence is a date and a count. |
| 479 | Haldane, "The Great Divide" (2016) https://www.bis.org/speeches/20160520-great-divide.pdf | "I would like to thank James Antwis, Andrew Bailey, Jeremy Franklin, Jonathan Fullwood…" | "Trust did not butter our parsnips and nor did it enter our production functions." | "But having seen it eroded, it now falls to us all to rebuild it, brick by brick, bank by bank…" | A kitchen idiom next to "production functions": the joke is the clash of registers. The gap between finance and the public may be finance not understanding the public. |
| 480 | Haldane, "The big fish small pond problem" (2011) https://www.bis.org/speeches/20110413-big-fish-small-pond-problem.pdf | "Global capital markets are engaged in a footrace." | "Where once it was raspberries, today's policymakers are blowing kisses." | "What is at stake may be more than just the future stability of the international financial system." | **A five-word thesis as the first sentence.** One playful image per paragraph, then the serious close. |
| 481 | Haldane, "Financial arms races" (2012) https://www.bis.org/speeches/20120426-financial-arms-races.pdf | "Elephant seals have got too big for their beaches." | "No longer: the sleek have inherited the earth." | "Fortunately, there is." | An absurd first line that the speech then justifies; a three-word close. Liquidity that looks deep can vanish under stress (about 60 cancelled orders per trade; the 2010 flash crash). |
| 482 | Haldane, "Whose Recovery?" (2016) https://www.bis.org/speeches/20160719-whose-recovery.pdf | "It is a great pleasure to be here in Port Talbot." | "When the banks sneezed in 2008, the whole economy caught a cold – and atishoo, atishoo, we all fell down." | "Thank you." | "Yes, I know we should get out more." Quotes a real person's question ("What exactly do you mean by recovery?") and lets it drive the speech. **F1.** |
| 483 | D.J. Taylor, "Orwell and the Toads" (Orwell Foundation) https://orwellfoundation.substack.com/p/orwell-and-the-toads | "One fail-safe route into Orwell's relish of the natural world is his essay 'Some Thoughts on the Common Toad', first published in Tribune in April 1946." | Orwell's list: "Roach, rudd, dace, bleak, barbel, bream, gudgeon, pike, chub, carp, tench." | "As for the specimen currently being bred up at Barnhill, 'we cannot wait for him to go to the butcher'" | A list of names as a sentence: the nouns do the feeling. Ends on Orwell's own deflating line about a pig. |

| 497 | "And the Secret Ingredient is…" (2022) https://apply.jhu.edu/hopkins-insider/and-the-secret-ingredient-is/ | "On the granite countertop in front of me sat a pile of flour, two sticks of butter, …" | "Collaborative endeavors are the proving grounds for Murphy's Law: everything that can go wrong, will go wrong." | "Enjoy!" | **The close is one word, addressed to the reader**, in the voice of a recipe. Teamwork told as a kitchen comedy. |
| 498 | "Superheroes" (2022) https://apply.jhu.edu/hopkins-insider/superheroes/ | "Legends, lore, and comic books all feature mystical, beautiful beings and superheroes" | "Sometimes the moments that speak the loudest aren't the noisiest or the most energetic." | "See, that's the kind of world-saving I do." | The close shrinks the title to the writer's size, with a wink. |
| 499 | "Learning How to Play" (2022) https://apply.jhu.edu/hopkins-insider/learning-how-to-play/ | "The first board game I ever played was Disney Princess Monopoly against my mother." | "Through them, I discovered what it really means to fight to win." | "…I've noticed my mother paying much more attention to my moves and habits and even learning a few things from me." | A comic first line; the close reverses the opening (the mother now learns). |
| 500 | "And on That Note" (2022) https://apply.jhu.edu/hopkins-insider/and-on-that-note/ | "The sound was loud and discordant, like a hurricane, high notes and low notes mixing together in an audible mess." | "But what I play, no matter how discordant, can be beautiful." | "It's my own unique polyphonic note." | The close returns to the opening sound (a callback). |
| 501 | "Time to Spin the Wheel" (2022) https://apply.jhu.edu/hopkins-insider/time-to-spin-the-wheel/ | "For as long as I can remember, one of my favorite pastimes has been manipulating those tricky permutations of 26 letters…" | ""I love you," whose incredible emotion is quantized to a mere set of eight letters, never cease to amaze me." | "Who knows, but I'm definitely game to give the wheel a spin, Pat, and see where it takes me…" | The close speaks to the host of the show by name. |

Batch 15 note: three of the eleven Hopkins essays end by speaking to someone ("Enjoy!", "Pat", "See, that's the
kind of world-saving I do."), and those are the closes that sound like a person. Haldane's best speeches open on an
absurd sentence ("Elephant seals have got too big for their beaches.") and spend the speech earning it. Lamb and
Woolf turn from light to grave in one line.

Batch 16 note: the two Watsa pieces are the same man thirteen years apart. When he owns a loss ("your Chairman's
bright ideas!", $5 million written off) he sounds human; when he defends against a critic he sounds like a lawyer.
Bezos's best letters open on the thesis and close on a turn of the same words ("Owners are different from tenants."
… "Here's to not being a tenant!").

Batch 17 note: Bezos kept one refrain ("it's still Day 1") and one attachment (the 1997 letter) for over a decade,
and the letters with a small true story (the handstand, 523; "kicking our first party butt. Badly.", 524) are the
ones people quote. Jassy's letters (526-527) are just as clear, with no story and no joke, and they read like
reports. Chesterton and Lamb show the essayists' version of synthesis 3's first point: open on something that sounds
trivial (two boys in a front garden, a piece of chalk, a pig) and make it carry the whole argument.

| 552 | Emerson, "Compensation" (1841; partial) https://gutenberg.org/cache/epub/16643/pg16643-images.html | (not in the part read) | "Every sweet hath its sour; every evil its good." | (not in the part read) | "For everything you have missed, you have gained something else; and for everything you gain, you lose something." The trade-off as a law of nature: every plan's cost is the shadow of its benefit. |
| 553 | Stevenson, "Virginibus Puerisque" I (1881) https://www.gutenberg.org/cache/epub/386/pg386-images.html | "With the single exception of Falstaff, all Shakespeare's characters are what we call marrying men." | "It is not taste that is plentiful, but courage that is rare." | "For marriage is like life in this—that it is a field of battle, and not a bed of roses." | A sweeping, slightly absurd first line; a sober close. |
| 554 | Stevenson, "Crabbed Age and Youth" (same volume; close not read) | "There is a strong feeling in favour of cowardly and prudential proverbs." | "Prudence is a wooden juggernaut, before whom Benjamin Franklin walks with the portly air of a high priest." | (cut off) | **A counter-voice for Caplet:** the case against prudence, made with wit. Useful to argue with in the Final Report. |
| 555 | Chesterton, "The Case for the Ephemeral" (All Things Considered, 1908) https://www.gutenberg.org/cache/epub/11505/pg11505-images.html | "I cannot understand the people who take literature seriously; but I can love them, and I do." | "It is so easy to be solemn; it is so hard to be frivolous." | "It will matter to us greatly on what side we fought." | Mocks his own book as hurried, then turns serious in the last line. **F6, then the turn.** |
| 556 | Chesterton, "Cockneys and Their Jokes" (same volume) | "A writer in the Yorkshire Evening Post is very angry indeed with my performances in this column." | "I believe firmly in the value of all vulgar notions, especially of vulgar jokes." | "…we shall surely find it, not in the literature which studies the people, but in the literature which the people studies." | Opens on a critic's attack and answers it; the close is a chiasmus. |
| 557 | Chesterton, "Woman" (same volume) | "A correspondent has written me an able and interesting letter in the matter of some allusions of mine…" | "Let woman be more of a maker, not less." | "Woman represents the despotic." | Long playful set-ups, then a blunt short verdict. (Dated views; read for the shape only.) |

### 558-571 · The RBA, 2021-22: Lowe, Ellis, Bullock, Kent, Debelle · speeches
| # | Speech | Open | Best line | Close | Craft · Insight |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 558 | Lowe, "Delta, the Economy and Monetary Policy" (Sep 2021) https://www.rba.gov.au/speeches/2021/sp-gov-2021-09-14.html | "Thank you for joining us today to support the Anika Foundation." | "Whether the same will be the case this time remains to be seen." | "I am happy to answer your questions." | A pun on the virus in the title; the rest is careful. |
| 559 | Lowe, "From Recovery to Expansion" (Toowoomba, Jun 2021) https://www.rba.gov.au/speeches/2021/sp-gov-2021-06-17.html | "Thank you for the invitation to join the Australian Farm Institute's conference." | "It is time, though, to be thinking about how we transition from recovery mode to expansion mode." | "I look forward to your questions." | A control sample. |
| 560 | Lowe, "The RBA and the Australian Economy" (Wagga Wagga, Dec 2021) https://www.rba.gov.au/speeches/2021/sp-gov-2021-12-16.html | "I would like to thank the CPA for inviting me to your Riverina Forum." | "I spent many hours on cold winter mornings and hot summer afternoons pumping petrol." | "I am happy to answer your questions." | **His home town, and one specific memory**, inside an otherwise careful speech. Synthesis 4, point 4 again. |
| 561 | Ellis, "Lessons and Lasting Effects of the Pandemic" (Jun 2021) https://www.rba.gov.au/speeches/2021/sp-ag-2021-06-23.html | "It's wonderful to be here in Adelaide, and to able to speak to the Ai Group in person." | "Full employment is a worthy goal for its own sake, given how important jobs and income are for people's welfare." | "I look forward to any questions you might have." | "So policy support really matters." Four plain words after a long argument. |
| 562 | Ellis, "Innovation and Dynamism in the Post-pandemic World" (Nov 2021) https://www.rba.gov.au/speeches/2021/sp-ag-2021-11-18.html | "Thanks very much to the Committee for the Economic Development of Australia for the invitation to give this talk." | "In the dynamic realm of business, innovation and investment, though, it is more a case of nothing ventured, nothing gained." | "Thank you for your time." | A proverb used straight. |
| 563 | Debelle, "The Indigenous Economy in Australia and the Reserve Bank of Australia" (Nov 2021) https://www.rba.gov.au/speeches/2021/sp-dg-2021-11-30.html | "The Reserve Bank has a long engagement with First Nations peoples." | "There is, however, the opportunity to deepen our understanding of the First Nations people in the Australian economy" | "These actions are only a starting point in our engagement with Australia's First Nations people." | Institutional "we" throughout; sincere and flat. |
| 564 | Lowe at the Walkley Awards for Business Journalism (Mar 2022) https://www.rba.gov.au/speeches/2022/sp-gov-2022-03-22.html | "Well, it's clear that different setups can work." | "So if you get a mortgage at, say, 2½ per cent, the banks have to make sure you can pay that back at 5½%" | "We haven't done that badly." | Off-script again: an understated close. The mortgage line is a stress test in one sentence. |
| 565 | Lowe, remarks at the Board dinner, Hobart (Nov 2022) https://www.rba.gov.au/speeches/2022/sp-gov-2022-11-01.html | "Good evening." | "How much things can change in just a few short years." | "We are looking forward to learning more from you over dinner about how things are going here in Tasmania." | Said in the middle of the rate rises he had said were unlikely before 2024. The wistful line does the admitting. |
| 566 | Lowe, "Price Stability, the Supply Side and Prosperity" (Nov 2022) https://www.rba.gov.au/speeches/2022/sp-gov-2022-11-22.html | "It is an honour to be able to continue the tradition of the RBA Governor addressing CEDA's Annual Dinner." | "Rather, the reverse was true. High inflation meant lower growth, fewer jobs and lower real wages." | "I am happy to answer your questions." | "A strong nominal anchor is more important than ever." Corrects a myth in two sentences. |
| 567 | Bullock, "How Are Households Placed for Interest Rate Increases?" (Jul 2022) https://www.rba.gov.au/speeches/2022/sp-dg-2022-07-19.html | "Thank you for the opportunity to speak with you today." | "Large buffers allow households to smooth their spending and maintain required debt payments" | "Thank you and I look forward to your questions." | The title is the question the audience is asking. Buffers as the answer to rising rates. |
| 568 | Bullock, "Policymaking at the Reserve Bank" (Oct 2022) https://www.rba.gov.au/speeches/2022/sp-dg-2022-10-18.html | "Thank you for the opportunity to address AFIA's conference." | "What used to be regarded as the boring plumbing of the economy is now at the forefront of innovation." | "Thank you for your attention." | "You should be in no doubt, though, that the Board is determined to do what is necessary to return inflation to target." Flat certainty about the policy, with no forecast attached (synthesis 4, point 2). |
| 569 | Kent, "From QE to QT" (May 2022) https://www.rba.gov.au/speeches/2022/sp-ag-2022-05-23.html | "It is great to be here in person." | "In short, the market would probably assume 'once a seller, always a seller'." | "Most importantly though, the Bank will continue to be able to maintain effective control over the cash rate…" | A title of two acronyms. Letting bonds run off at maturity makes the unwind predictable; selling would signal more selling. |
| 570 | Kent, "Exchange Rates and Inflationary Pressures" (Oct 2022) https://www.rba.gov.au/speeches/2022/sp-ag-2022-10-24.html | "I'd like to thank CBA for the opportunity to be here today." | "Because much of global trade is invoiced in US dollars, this will add to the cost of imports for a time." | "…will contribute only a very modest uplift in the level of consumer prices over the period ahead." | The AUD fell about 14% against the USD but only about 2% trade-weighted: what a currency move costs depends on what you buy with it. |
| 571 | Ellis, "The Neutral Rate: The Pole-star Casts Faint Light" (Oct 2022) https://www.rba.gov.au/speeches/2022/sp-ag-2022-10-12.html | "Thanks to Citi for the opportunity to speak today." | "'Neutral', then, is not a destination we necessarily reach, but more a pole-star to guide us." | "Thank you for your time." | **One metaphor, in the title and once in the body**, that tells the audience how to use an uncertain number: steer by it, don't aim for it. "the stars themselves can realign." |

Batch 18 note: the central bankers' best lines are about how to hold an uncertain number (Ellis's pole-star, 571;
Lowe's 2½ per cent tested at 5½, 564), and the human ones are a single memory (pumping petrol in Wagga, 560). Jassy's
2025 letter shows a voice can change inside one writer: the year he opened on his own failed plan, the letter came
alive.

## Round 2, batch 19 (28 Oct, 16:40-16:52 Sydney): 572-600
Checked against the log's URL and title list first. Not counted: The Spectator, Volume 1 on Gutenberg (both halves held
only the editor's introduction, not No. 1 or No. 2); the second half of Emerson's "Essays, First Series" (read only to
finish "Self-Reliance", counted once as 580); Gutenberg book pages and the RBA's 2020 and 2023 speech lists (used only
to find URLs). Quotes marked "…" were cut at the reader's 125-character limit.

|---|---|---|---|---|---|

### 580-581, 587 · Emerson and Twain · essays
| # | Piece | Open | Best line | Close | Craft |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 580 | Emerson, "Self-Reliance" (1841) https://www.gutenberg.org/cache/epub/2944/pg2944-images.html | "I read the other day some verses written by an eminent painter which were original and not conventional." | "A foolish consistency is the hobgoblin of little minds, adored by little statesmen and philosophers and divines." | "Nothing can bring you peace but the triumph of principles." | **The sermon that made a career out of "trust thyself"**, opening on something he happened to read. "Trust thyself: every heart vibrates to that iron string." Lines short enough to carve. |
| 581 | Twain, "Corn-pone Opinions" (1901) https://paulgraham.com/cornpone.html | "FIFTY YEARS AGO, when I was a boy of fifteen and helping to inhabit a Missourian village on the banks of the Mississippi,…" | "We all do no end of feeling, and we mistake it for thinking." | "Some think it the Voice of God." | "helping to inhabit" is the joke in the first line. "Broadly speaking, there are none but corn-pone opinions." A thesis that admits it includes the writer. Five-word kicker. |
| 587 | Twain, "On the Decay of the Art of Lying" (1880) https://www.gutenberg.org/cache/epub/2572/pg2572-images.html | "Observe, I do not mean to suggest that the custom of lying has suffered any decay or interruption…" | "I think that all this courteous lying is a sweet and loving art, and should be cultivated." | "…a ripe body, who may be termed, in this regard, and without undue flattery, Old Masters." | Mock-solemn: an outrageous claim delivered as a club paper. "Everybody lies—every day; every hour; awake; asleep; in his dreams…" The list is the joke. |

### 582-586, 588-594 · RBA 2020, the pandemic year · speeches
| # | Piece | What it shows |
|---|---|---|
| 582 | Lowe, "Responding to the Economic and Financial Impact of COVID-19" (19 Mar 2020) https://www.rba.gov.au/speeches/2020/sp-gov-2020-03-19.html | Opens "Good afternoon." Two words, in the worst week. "Undeniably, what we are facing today is a very serious situation, but it is something that is temporary." **F5, dread then relief, in one sentence.** "At some point, the virus will be contained and our economy and our financial markets will recover." |
| 583 | Bullock, "Panic, Pandemic and Payment Preferences" (Jun 2020) https://www.rba.gov.au/speeches/2020/sp-ag-2020-06-03.html | Alliterative title on a dry subject. "Some of the sharp and sudden shift to contactless and mobile payments, and away from cash is likely to be permanent." |
| 584 | Debelle, "The Virus and the Australian Economy" (11 Mar 2020) https://www.rba.gov.au/speeches/2020/sp-dg-2020-03-11.html | "The virus is a shock to both demand and supply." Nine words that frame the whole crisis. "It is just too uncertain to assess the impact of the virus beyond the March quarter." Says where the forecast stops. |
| 585 | Lowe, "The Recovery from a Very Uneven Recession" (Oct 2020) https://www.rba.gov.au/speeches/2020/sp-gov-2020-10-15.html | "All recessions are uneven, but this one has been especially so." A general truth, then the exception. |
| 586 | Kent, "The Stance of Monetary Policy in a World of Numerous Tools" (Oct 2020) https://www.rba.gov.au/speeches/2020/sp-ag-2020-10-20.html | "In a world of unconventional policies, assessing the stance of monetary policy is not as straight forward as it once was." "Because banks are generally flush with cash, they don't need to compete very hard to raise short-term funds." |
| 588 | Lowe, "An Economic and Financial Update" (Apr 2020) https://www.rba.gov.au/speeches/2020/sp-gov-2020-04-21.html | A recurring "bridge" metaphor across the pandemic. "We can be confident that our economy will bounce back and that we will see it recover." |
| 589 | Lowe, "The Year Ahead" (5 Feb 2020) https://www.rba.gov.au/speeches/2020/sp-gov-2020-02-05.html | Six weeks before 582. The virus is one uncertainty among several, with SARS as the guide. "Strong fundamentals, though, can take us only so far." **Read 589 then 582: the forecast is the thing that breaks.** |
| 590 | Ellis, "The Economic Outlook" (Aug 2020) https://www.rba.gov.au/speeches/2020/sp-ag-2020-08-07.html | "The COVID-19 pandemic has been the most profound shock to the economy and society in many decades." Then, of the data: "This has been a surprising outcome of the current episode." |
| 591 | Debelle, "Monetary Policy in 2020" (Nov 2020) https://www.rba.gov.au/speeches/2020/sp-dg-2020-11-24.html | "The strong capital buffers of the banks are there to be used, not preserved." **A buffer is for spending in the bad year**, said in eleven words. |
| 592 | Lowe, "COVID-19, the Labour Market and Public Sector Balance Sheets" (Jul 2020) https://www.rba.gov.au/speeches/2020/sp-gov-2020-07-21.html | "The reality, though, is there is no free lunch." Then: "The message here is that somebody always pays." Two cliché-plain lines that land because the rest is careful. |
| 593 | Lowe, "Today's Monetary Policy Decision" (Nov 2020) https://www.rba.gov.au/speeches/2020/sp-gov-2020-11-03.html | Opens "Good afternoon." and closes "Thank you." "It is not enough for inflation to be forecast to be in the target range." A rule that refuses to trust its own forecast. |
| 594 | Kent, "The Reserve Bank's Operations – Liquidity, Market Function and Funding" (Jul 2020) https://www.rba.gov.au/speeches/2020/sp-ag-2020-07-27.html | March 2020: "we faced the extremely unusual situation whereby prices of risky and risk-free assets were both falling." Holders had to sell bonds for cash. **The safest asset fell when people were forced to sell it.** |

### 595-600 · RBA 2023, the reckoning · speeches
| # | Piece | What it shows |
|---|---|---|
| 595 | Lowe, "Some Closing Remarks" (his farewell, Sep 2023) https://www.rba.gov.au/speeches/2023/sp-gov-2023-09-07.html | **The admission, finally said plainly**: "With the benefit of hindsight, my view is that we did do too much." "We got some things right, but we got other things wrong." "I know that some of my explanations have missed the mark." Close: "I look forward to answering your questions for one final time." The familiar closing line, with three words added, becomes the goodbye. |
| 596 | Lowe, "A Narrow Path" (Jun 2023) https://www.rba.gov.au/speeches/2023/sp-gov-2023-06-07.html | A two-word title that becomes a phrase. "But it is a narrow path and likely to be a bumpy one, with risks on both sides." |
| 597 | Jones, "Bagehot and the Lender of Last Resort – 150 Years On" (Dec 2023) https://www.rba.gov.au/speeches/2023/sp-ag-2023-12-14.html | Opens "Good morning." "Put simply, the next major shock is a matter of when, not if." A 150-year-old book used as the frame. |
| 598 | Kent, "Long and Variable Monetary Policy Lags" (Mar 2023) https://www.rba.gov.au/speeches/2023/sp-ag-2023-03-20.html | Friedman's phrase as the title, uncited. "It's great to be back at the KangaNews Summit." "The lags in the transmission of policy are not only long, but they are variable" |
| 599 | Lowe, "Inflation and Recent Economic Data" (Mar 2023) https://www.rba.gov.au/speeches/2023/sp-gov-2023-03-08.html | "High inflation is corrosive and damaging." Five words, then the cost: "If we don't get inflation down fairly soon, the end result will be even higher interest rates and more unemployment." |
| 600 | Lowe, "Monetary Policy, Demand and Supply" (Apr 2023) https://www.rba.gov.au/speeches/2023/sp-gov-2023-04-05.html | The pause: "The decision to hold rates steady this month does not imply that interest rate increases are over." The phrase is already here, two months before 596: "It is a narrow path, though, and there are other plausible scenarios." |

Batch 19 note: the RBA 2019-2023 run reads as one story in four acts. Patient (573, 589), the forecast breaks (582, 584),
soul-searching (572), and the farewell: "we did do too much" (595). The plainer the admission, the more it lands: Lowe's
plain sentence in 595 beats Bullock's careful conditionals in 574. The essays add two tricks. Twain makes the thesis include
himself (581). Emerson opens on something he read the other day (580), a small real moment before the big claim.

### 601-610 · RBA 2023, the year after the reckoning · speeches
| # | Piece | What it shows |
|---|---|---|
| 601 | Lowe, remarks at the Board dinner, Perth (May 2023) https://www.rba.gov.au/speeches/2023/sp-gov-2023-05-02.html | "Good evening." Then, on the $100 notes on issue: "I don't have my share and I don't know many people who do." **A self-deprecating joke about a statistic** turns a number into a laugh. Then the serious line: "History teaches us that it is difficult to bring inflation down while keeping the economy on an even keel." |
| 602 | Lowe, "Reserve Bank Review" (Apr 2023) https://www.rba.gov.au/speeches/2023/sp-gov-2023-04-20.html | Accepting a review of his own bank: "From a number of perspectives, current oversight arrangements fall short of contemporary standards." Then: "But as times change, we need to change too." |
| 603 | Lowe, "The Reserve Bank Review and Monetary Policy" (Jul 2023) https://www.rba.gov.au/speeches/2023/sp-gov-2023-07-12.html | "High inflation hurts all Australians and damages the functioning of the economy." "Our priority remains to ensure this period of high inflation is only temporary." Plain and safe; nothing to remember. |
| 604 | Bullock, "A Monetary Policy Fit for the Future" (Nov 2023) https://www.rba.gov.au/speeches/2023/sp-gov-2023-11-22.html | "But as times change, we must change." **Almost word for word Lowe's line in 602**, seven months later. An institution's voice outlives the speaker. |
| 605 | Bullock, "Monetary Policy in Australia: Complementarities and Trade-offs" (Oct 2023, first speech as Governor) https://www.rba.gov.au/speeches/2023/sp-gov-2023-10-24.html | "Good evening." "Over time, low inflation and full employment go hand in hand." A trade-off that isn't one over the long run: the short-term cost is named, then the long-term match. |
| 606 | Bullock, "Climate Change and Central Banks" (Aug 2023) https://www.rba.gov.au/speeches/2023/sp-dg-2023-08-29.html | "Climate change, however, is a trend change." Six words; the same trend-versus-cycle line as Debelle in 578, four years on. |
| 607 | Bullock, "Achieving Full Employment" (Newcastle, Jun 2023) https://www.rba.gov.au/speeches/2023/sp-dg-2023-06-20.html | "Full employment is, and has always been, one of our two main objectives." Grounded in the room: Newcastle's unemployment rate of about 3 per cent. |
| 608 | Jones, "A Tokenised Future for the Australian Financial System?" (Oct 2023) https://www.rba.gov.au/speeches/2023/sp-ag-2023-10-16.html | **The question mark in the title is the verdict.** "The punchline here is that tokenisation offers some intriguing possibilities, but is not without its challenges" Says "punchline" and then gives one. |
| 609 | Jones, "Emerging Threats to Financial Stability – New Challenges for the Next Decade" (Oct 2023) https://www.rba.gov.au/speeches/2023/sp-ag-2023-10-31.html | Opens on a curse: "'May you live in interesting times' is a sardonic curse with uncertain origin." Closes on the reply: "But we can be better prepared for whatever might come our way." **F5 in the first and last lines.** |
| 610 | Kent, "Channels of Transmission" (Oct 2023) https://www.rba.gov.au/speeches/2023/sp-ag-2023-10-11.html | "The cash-flow channel is felt acutely by those with variable-rate debt." Who feels the policy, said in one line. |

| 617 | Kent, "Benchmark Reforms" (Nov 2020) https://www.rba.gov.au/speeches/2020/sp-ag-2020-11-17.html | "The end of LIBOR is a certainty." "Fallbacks provide important insurance." Two short lines carry an urgent technical speech. |

Batch 20 note: the best lines in forty RBA speeches are jokes or confessions told against the speaker's own
institution: black swans in Perth (623), the missing $100 notes (601), "we have done pretty well" (613). The
institution's own voice repeats itself ("as times change", 602 and 604), so a phrase that belongs to one person stands
out. Ellis ends a speech about lags by giving the audience its time back (618): the last line is about the room, which
is synthesis 4's fix for weak closes. Lowe's "up, not down" (628) is the 2018 entry in the record of forecast misses
(573, 572, 595).

### 640-643 · Thoreau and Johnson · essays
| # | Piece | Open | Best line | Close | Craft |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 640 | Thoreau, "Walking" (1862) https://gutenberg.org/cache/epub/1022/pg1022-images.html | "I wish to speak a word for Nature, for absolute Freedom and Wildness, as contrasted with a freedom and culture…" | "Life consists with wildness. The most alive is the wildest." | "So we saunter toward the Holy Land, till one day the sun shall shine more brightly than ever he has done…" | **Opens by announcing a side and keeps it.** "I believe in the forest, and in the meadow, and in the night in which the corn grows." A creed as a list of places. |
| 641 | Johnson, The Adventurer No. 39, "On Sleep" (1753) https://gutenberg.org/cache/epub/12050/pg12050-images.html | "If every day did not produce fresh instances of the ingratitude of mankind, we might, perhaps, be at a loss…" | "Sleep is necessary to the happy to prevent satiety, and to endear life by a short absence" | "…wise is he that remembers of both, that they can be safe and happy only by virtue." | Balanced Latinate clauses; the antithesis is the engine. |
| 642 | Johnson, The Adventurer No. 50, "On Lying" (1753), same volume | "When Aristotle was once asked, what a man could gain by uttering falsehoods?" | "The liar, and only the liar, is invariably and universally despised, abandoned, and disowned" | "…nor feel guilt, but as they dread punishment." | Opens on a question someone else was asked. Read with Twain's 587 on the same subject: Johnson condemns, Twain pretends to praise, and Twain is the one remembered. |
| 643 | Johnson, The Adventurer No. 67 (1753), same volume | "That familiarity produces neglect, has been long observed." | "Happiness is enjoyed only in proportion as it is known." | "…he gains leisure for intellectual pleasures, and enjoys the happiness of reason and reflection." | Eight-word opener, a maxim. "the knowledge that something remains yet unenjoyed, impairs our enjoyment of the good before us." |

Batch 21 note: the speeches that move people do one of two things in the first minute. They tell a small true story
(Bezos's ranch, 646; Adichie's roommate, 645; Bogle's party, 644), or they say the audience's objection out loud ("My
title must seem like a contradiction", 649; "why the speaker is so old", 657). Three of the best (644, 651, 667) hang on
one word or object that returns at the end. Two use inversion as the whole structure (656, 661), which is a way to be
funny and serious at once. Johnson's and Twain's essays on lying (642, 587) are the clearest pair yet: the one that
pretends is the one people remember.

| 679 | Lamb, "Detached Thoughts on Books and Reading" (1822) https://essays.quotidiana.org/lamb/detached_thoughts_on_books/ | "An ingenious acquaintance of my own was so much struck with this bright sally of his Lordship, that he has…" | "I dream away my life in others' speculations." | "No wonder if he wish he ne'er had learn'd to eat." | "Books think for me." Three words, a confession. |
| 680 | Hazlitt, "On Familiar Style" (1821) https://essays.quotidiana.org/hazlitt/familiar_style/ | "It is not easy to write a familiar style." | "It is not to take the first word that offers, but the best word in common use;" | "Their Temple of Fame is like a shadow structure raised by Dulness to Vanity…" | **The plain-style manifesto, two centuries before plain-English guides**: "To write a genuine familiar or truly English style, is to write as any one would speak in common conversation…" |
| 681 | Hazlitt, "On the Past and Future" (1822) https://essays.quotidiana.org/hazlitt/past_and_future/ | "I have naturally but little imagination, and am not of a very sanguine turn of mind." | "The future is like a dead wall or a thick mist hiding all objects from our view;" | "We are afraid to dwell upon the past, lest it should retard our future progress; the indulgence of ease is…" | F6 in the first sentence. "The past is alive and stirring with objects, bright or solemn, and of unfading interest." |
| 682 | Hazlitt, "On People with One Idea" (1821) https://essays.quotidiana.org/hazlitt/people_with_one_idea/ | "There are people who have but one idea: at least, if they have more, they keep it a secret, for they never talk…" | "He wears the Categories round his neck like a pearl-chain" | "In short, as a lover (talk of what you will) brings in his mistress at every turn, so these persons contrive…" | The first sentence's afterthought ("at least…") is the joke. **A warning for a one-motif IPS**: the man with one idea "brings in his mistress at every turn". |
| 683 | Hazlitt, "On a Sun-Dial" (1827) https://essays.quotidiana.org/hazlitt/sun-dial/ | "Horas non numero nisi serenas [I don't count the hours unless they're tranquil]—is the motto of a sundial near Venice." | "It is the shadow thrown across, that gives us warning of their flight." | "so I had better let it go as it is." | Opens on an inscription he saw; closes by giving up, wryly. |
| 684 | Hazlitt, "On Will-Making" (1821) https://essays.quotidiana.org/hazlitt/willmaking/ | "Few things show the human character in a more ridiculous light than the circumstance of will-making." | "Men like to collect money into large heaps in their lifetime; they like to leave it in large heaps after they are dead." | "…and go out of the world in the same state of stupid amazement that we came into it!" | "It is not the errors of others, but our own miscalculations, on which we wreak our lasting vengeance." |
| 685 | Hazlitt, "On Thought and Action" (1821) https://essays.quotidiana.org/hazlitt/thought_and_action/ | "Those persons who are much accustomed to abstract contemplation are generally unfitted for active pursuits, and vice-versa." | "Great thoughts reduced to practice become great acts." | "The story suggests the idea of a magician; nor is there anything in the Arabian Nights that looks more like a fiction." | Confesses his own awkwardness before he generalises. |
| 686 | Hazlitt, "A Farewell to Essay-Writing" (1828) https://essays.quotidiana.org/hazlitt/farewell_to_essay_writing/ | "Food, warmth, sleep, and a book; these are all I at present ask—the ultima Thule of my wandering desires." | "To get others to come into our ways of thinking, we must go over to theirs" | "And curtain-close such scene from every future view." | **Opens on a list of four nouns**, like Lamb's 678. The persuasion line is the whole of synthesis 4, point 5 (translate yourself). |
| 687 | Hazlitt, "On the Pleasure of Hating" (1826) https://essays.quotidiana.org/hazlitt/pleasure_of_hating/ | "There is a spider crawling along the matted floor of the room where I sit…" | "We hate old friends: we hate old books: we hate old opinions; and at last we come to hate ourselves." | "Indeed I do; and chiefly for not having hated and despised the world enough." | **F1: a spider on his floor**, then the essay. The colon-chain builds to a turn on himself. |

### 688-699 · Addison, Johnson, Seneca, Kenko, Osler, Hume · essays (via https://essays.quotidiana.org/)
| # | Piece | What it shows |
|---|---|---|
| 688 | Addison, "The Adventures of a Shilling" (Tatler, 1710) https://essays.quotidiana.org/addison/adventures_of_a_shilling/ | **A coin tells its own story**: "for, as I told you before, we shillings love nothing so much as traveling." Money given a voice and a biography. |
| 689 | Addison, "On Westminster Abbey" (Spectator, 1711) https://essays.quotidiana.org/addison/westminster_abbey/ | "When I am in a serious Humour, I very often walk by my self in Westminster Abbey…" "When I see Kings lying by those who deposed them," Gloom turned to calm. |
| 690 | Johnson, The Idler No. 1 (1758) https://essays.quotidiana.org/johnson/idler_no_1/ | **Opens on the trouble of finding a title.** "Every man is, or hopes to be, an Idler." Close: "praise is not so welcome to the Idler as quiet." |
| 691 | Johnson, The Rambler No. 184 (1751) https://essays.quotidiana.org/johnson/rambler_no_184/ | On chance: "Since life itself is uncertain, nothing which has life for its basis can boast much stability." Fortune as "a subtle and insidious power, who will intrude upon privacy and embarrass caution." |
| 692 | Johnson, "A Journey on a Stage Coach" (Adventurer No. 84, 1753) https://essays.quotidiana.org/johnson/journey_on_a_stage_coach/ | Passengers in a coach each pretending to be grander than they are. "It is always observable that silence propagates itself" "Every man deceives himself while he thinks he is deceiving others" |
| 693 | Seneca, "Some Arguments in Favor of the Simple Life" (Letter 87) https://essays.quotidiana.org/seneca/arguments_in_favor_of_the_simple_life/ | **"I was shipwrecked before I got aboard."** Six words, a whole first line. "Money tumbles into the hands of certain men as a shilling tumbles down a sewer." Close: "If we can, let us speak more boldly; if not, let us speak more frankly." |
| 694 | Osler, "The Student Life" (1905) https://essays.quotidiana.org/osler/student_life/ | "Except it be a lover, no one is more interesting as an object of study than a student." "Thoroughness is the most difficult habit to acquire, but it is the pearl of great price…" |
| 695 | Hume, "Of Essay Writing" (1742) https://essays.quotidiana.org/hume/essay_writing/ | The essayist as an ambassador between learning and conversation, carried through as one diplomatic metaphor. Close: "…as to sacrifice the Substance to the Shadow." |
| 696 | Kenko, Essays in Idleness (selections, c. 1330) https://essays.quotidiana.org/kenko/selections/ | "In all things it is the beginning and end that are interesting." Fragments, no transitions; the gaps do the work. "So, though the dry flats extend far out, soon the tide comes and floods the beach." |
| 697 | Seneca, "On Scipio's Villa" (Letter 86) https://essays.quotidiana.org/seneca/scipios_villa/ | F1: "I am resting at the country-house which once belonged to Scipio Africanus himself;…" The bath-house shows the man: "But they smelled of the camp, the farm, and heroism." Close: "Farewell." |
| 698 | Addison, "On the Essay Form" (Spectator No. 124, 1711) https://essays.quotidiana.org/addison/essay_form/ | **The 500-word IPS, described in 1711**: "An Essay-Writer must practise in the Chymical Method, and give the Virtue of a full Draught in a few Drops." "many a bulky Author would make his Appearance in a Penny-Paper" |
| 699 | Johnson, The Idler No. 3 (1758) https://essays.quotidiana.org/johnson/idler_no_3/ | "There are certainly miseries in idleness, which the Idler only can conceive." "Much mischief is done in the world with very little interest or design." |

Batch 22 note: the old essayists open on one small thing in the room (Hazlitt's spider, 687; Seneca's villa, 697;
Addison's walk in the Abbey, 689) or a list of plain nouns ("Food, warmth, sleep, and a book", 686; "A clear fire, a clean
hearth", 678), and the big claim comes after. Addison's "a full Draught in a few Drops" (698) is the IPS's job in one
line, and Hazlitt's man who "brings in his mistress at every turn" (682) is the warning against overusing the motif. Two
pieces give money a voice or a race (688, 676); both are funny because they treat it with mock dignity.

## Round 2, batch 23 (28 Oct, 21:09-21:12 Sydney): 700-737
Checked against the log's URL and title list first. All from Quotidiana (https://essays.quotidiana.org/), read whole.
Not counted: four author index pages (Twain, Milne, Jerome, Belloc; used only to find URLs). Quotes marked "…" were cut
at the reader's 125-character limit.

### 700-711 · Hazlitt and Lamb, the rest · essays
| # | Piece | Open | Best line | Close | Craft |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 700 | Hazlitt, "On Gusto" (1816) https://essays.quotidiana.org/hazlitt/gusto/ | "Gusto in art is power or passion defining any object." | "Not only do his heads seem to think—his bodies seem to feel." | "If it is not, we are altogether mistaken in our notions on this delicate subject." | **A definition as the first line**, then examples. The close bets the whole essay on itself. |
| 701 | Hazlitt, "The Indian Jugglers" (1821) https://essays.quotidiana.org/hazlitt/indian_jugglers/ | "Coming forward and seating himself on the ground in his white dress and tightened turban…" | "It is skill surmounting difficulty, and beauty triumphing over skill." | "We have paid this willing tribute to his memory." | F1: opens mid-act on a juggler. The essay ends on an obituary for a fives player, a turn nobody sees coming. |
| 702 | Hazlitt, "On the Fear of Death" (1822) https://essays.quotidiana.org/hazlitt/fear_of_death/ | "Perhaps the best cure for the fear of death is to reflect that life has a beginning as well as an end." | "We think, how we should feel, not how the dead feel." | "If we merely wish to continue on the scene to indulge our headstrong humours and tormenting passions…" | One symmetry carries the essay: we don't mourn the years before we were born. |
| 703 | Hazlitt, "Why Distant Objects Please" (1821) https://essays.quotidiana.org/hazlitt/why_distant_objects_please/ | "Distant objects please, because, in the first place, they imply an idea of space and magnitude, and because, not being…" | "We drink the air before us, and borrow a more refined existence from objects that hover on the brink of nothing." | "This was truly and finely said long ago, by one who knew the strong and weak points of human nature; but it is what…" | The title asks the question; the first word answers it. |
| 704 | Hazlitt, "My First Acquaintance with Poets" (1823) https://essays.quotidiana.org/hazlitt/my_first_acquaintance/ | "My father was a Dissenting Minister, at Wem, in Shropshire; and in the year 1798 (the figures that compose the date …" | "He strides on so far before you, that he dwindles in the distance!" | "Enough of this for the present." | **A memoir that opens on a father, a town and a year.** The five-word close walks out of the room. |
| 705 | Hazlitt, "On the Difference between Writing and Speaking" (1826) https://essays.quotidiana.org/hazlitt/writing_and_speaking/ | "It is a common observation, that few persons can be found who speak and write equally well." | "the habit of writing is the habit of thinking aloud, but without the help of an echo." | "I fancy Mr. C. Wynne is the only person in the kingdom who has fully made up his mind that a total defect of voice …" | "The most dashing orator I ever heard is the flattest writer I ever read." **Writing has no audience to react; the writer must imagine one** (synthesis 4, point 7). |
| 706 | Hazlitt, "On Genius and Common Sense" (1821) https://essays.quotidiana.org/hazlitt/genius_and_common_sense/ | "We hear it maintained by people of more gravity than understanding, that genius and taste are strictly reducible to rules…" | "Reason is the interpreter and critic of nature and genius, not their law-giver and judge." | "Good nature and common sense are required from all people; but one proud distinction is enough…" | Insult in the first line ("more gravity than understanding"). |
| 707 | Hazlitt, "On Living to One's Self" (1821) https://essays.quotidiana.org/hazlitt/living_to_ones_self/ | "I was never in a better place or humour than I am at present for writing on this subject." | "He is free as air, and independent as the wind." | "…whilst my soul is thus raised up to heaven, it imports me little what ground I tread upon." | Opens on where he is sitting as he writes (a country inn). |
| 708 | Lamb, "A Chapter on Ears" (1821) https://essays.quotidiana.org/lamb/chapter_on_ears/ | "I have no ear.—" | "the purgatory of the Enraged Musician becomes my paradise." | "…and restores to me the genuine unterrifying aspects of my pleasant-countenanced host and hostess." | **Four words, a confession, then a dash.** The essay is a joke against himself (he means no ear for music). |
| 709 | Lamb, "Oxford in the Vacation" (1820) https://essays.quotidiana.org/lamb/oxford_in_the_vacation/ | "Casting a preparatory glance at the bottom of this article—as the wary connoisseur in prints, with cursory eye…" | "I can rise at the chapel-bell, and dream that it rings for me." | "When he goes about with you to show you the halls and colleges, you think you have with you…" | A clerk pretending to be a scholar for a week; the pretence is the pathos. |
| 710 | Lamb, "Popular Fallacies: That a Bully Is Always a Coward" (1826) https://essays.quotidiana.org/lamb/popular_fallacies_1/ | "This axiom contains a principle of compensation, which disposes us to admit the truth of it." | "Pretensions do not uniformly bespeak non-performance." | "This was true distributive justice." | **A proverb taken apart**: we believe it because we want it to be true. |
| 711 | Lamb, "The Old and the New Schoolmaster" (1821) https://essays.quotidiana.org/lamb/old_and_new_schoolmaster/ | "My reading has been lamentably desultory and immethodical." | "The trumpet does not more stun you by its loudness, than a whisper teases you by its provoking inaudibility." | "For the communication of this letter, I am indebted to my cousin Bridget." | Opens on his own ignorance, F6, before he mocks anyone else's. |

| # | Piece | What it shows |
|---|---|---|
| 722 | Twain, "The Art of Inhumation" (Life on the Mississippi, 1883) https://essays.quotidiana.org/twain/art_of_inhumation/ | **Satire by letting a salesman talk**: an undertaker praises his trade. | 726 | Milne, "The Pleasure of Writing" https://essays.quotidiana.org/milne/pleasure_of_writing/ | An essay about a new pen nib. **The last sentence repeats a line from the middle**, "That is the real pleasure of writing.", and lands harder the second time. |
| 727 | Milne, "Goldfish" https://essays.quotidiana.org/milne/goldfish/ | "Let us talk about—well, anything you will." The dash is the shrug. Close: "Which is all I want to say about goldfish." |
| 728 | Jerome, "On Being Idle" (Idle Thoughts of an Idle Fellow, 1886) https://essays.quotidiana.org/jerome/being_idle/ | "Idleness, like kisses, to be sweet must be stolen." "I like idling when I ought not to be idling; not when it is the only thing I have to do." |
